\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
i>>0
. Then,
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
(
Definition

3.17
) is a triangulated subcategory of
\mathsf{T}
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition (
Definition

3.17
) that
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is closed under suspensions and desuspensions. So, we just need to show that
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is closed under extensions. Let
F_{1},F_{2}\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
and
F\in\mathsf{T}
, such that there is a triangle
F_{1}\to F\to F_{2}\to\Sigma F_{1}
. Choose and fix any integer
n
. Then, by
Definition

3.17
, there exist
E_{2}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, and
D_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
such that
F_{2}\in E_{2}\star D_{2}
. By
Lemma

3.21
, there exists an integer
b
such that,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E_{2},\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}})=0
for all
i\geq n+b
. Then, again by
Definition

3.17
, there exist objects
E_{1}
and
D_{1}
such that
F_{1}\in E_{1}\star D_{1}
with
E_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{n+b+1}
. And so,
F\in E_{1}\star D_{1}\star E_{2}\star D_{2}
.

Now, let
C\in D_{1}\star E_{2}
, that is, there exists a triangle
\Sigma^{-1}E_{2}\to D_{1}\to C\to E_{2}
. Now, as
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{-1}E_{2},D_{1})=0
, we get that
C\cong D_{1}\oplus E_{2}\in E_{2}\star D_{1}
. And so,
D_{1}\star E_{2}=E_{2}\star D_{1}
. This gives us that,

F\in E_{1}\star E_{2}\star D_{1}\star D_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{T}^{c}\star\mathcal{R}_{n}

As this is true for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, and we are done.
∎

We now show that
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is thick for a
\mathcal{G}
-preapproximable triangulated category.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.5
), an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
), and an integer
A>0
satisfying the definition of a
\mathcal{G}
-preapproximable triangulated category, see
Definition

3.19
. Then,
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is a thick subcategory of
\mathsf{T}
.

Proof.

We already know that
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is triangulated by
Proposition

3.22
. We just need to show it is closed under direct summands. Let
F\oplus F^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. We want to show that
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. We first show that
F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We give the proof for
i\geq 0
, the proof for
i<0
goes similarly.

As
\operatorname{Cone}{(F\oplus F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{0\oplus 1}F\oplus F^{\prime})}\cong F\oplus\Sigma F
, we have that
F\oplus\Sigma F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
as
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is triangulated by
Proposition

3.22
, which gives us the claim for
i=0
.

Now suppose we know the claim up to some non-negative integer
i
, that is,
F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F
lies in
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. Further,
\Sigma^{2i+1}(F\oplus\Sigma F)\cong\Sigma^{2i+1}F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+2}F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. Then, by
Proposition

3.22
, we get that,
\operatorname{Cone}{(\Sigma^{2i+2}F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F\xrightarrow{0\oplus 1}F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F)}\cong F\oplus\Sigma^{2(i+1)+1}F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, and hence we get the induction step. So, we have shown that
F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now, fix an integer
n
. As by assumption
\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathcal{R}_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists an integer
i
such that
\Sigma^{2i+2}F\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
. We can show this as follows; there exists an integer
j
such that
\Sigma^{j}F\in\mathcal{R}_{n+1}
. But, as
\mathcal{R}
is an orthogonal metric, it is in particular an extended good metric, and so by
Definition

3.1
(2),
\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{n+1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{n+1}\cup\Sigma\mathcal{R}_{n+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{n}
. So, in particular,
\Sigma^{j}F,\Sigma^{j+1}F\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
, and
j
or
j+1
is even, which gives us the required
i
.

From the triangle,

F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F\to F\xrightarrow{0}\Sigma^{2i+2}F\to\Sigma F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+2}F

we get that,
F\in(F\oplus\Sigma^{2i+1}F)\star(\Sigma^{2i+2}F)\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}\star\mathcal{R}_{n}\subseteq\mathsf{T}^{c}\star\mathcal{R}_{n}
by
Definition

3.1
(1). As this is true for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
.
∎

4.
\mathcal{G}
-Approximability

We now give the main definitions of this work. We refer the reader to the introduction for some intuition for the following definition, see
Example

A
and
Idea

A
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(see
Definition

3.8
).
Then, we say
\mathsf{T}
is
weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable
if there exists an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(see
Definition

3.1
), and an integer
A>0
such that,

(1)

The triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
, the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
, and the integer
A>0
satisfy the definition of
\mathcal{G}
-preapproximability, see
Definition

3.19

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n+i\geq A
.

(3)

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}
(see
Notation

3.6
).

We say
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable
if we further have,

(3)
′

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}_{A}
(see
Notation

3.6
).

Note that condition (2) is similar too, but stronger than,
Definition

3.19
(2).

We also define a weaker notion, which will be enough to show the representability theorems. The only difference in this definition is that we only ask for objects in the closure of the compacts to be approximable.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(see
Definition

3.8
)
Then, we say
\mathsf{T}
is
weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable
if there exists an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(see
Definition

3.1
), and an integer
A>0
such that,

(1)

The triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
, the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
, and the integer
A>0
satisfy the definition of
\mathcal{G}
-preapproximability, see
Definition

3.19
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n+i\geq A
.

(3)

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}
.

We say
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable
if we further have,

(3)
′

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}_{A}
.

It will follow from
Proposition

5.3
that any (weakly)
\mathcal{G}
-approximable triangulated category is (weakly)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable.

Remark 4.3
.

For the ease of the reader, we explicitly write down the conditions required in the definition of
\mathcal{G}
-approximability. Note that analogous statements hold for
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximability.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(see
Definition

3.8
)
Then,
\mathsf{T}
is
weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable
if there exists an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(see
Definition

3.1
), and an integer
A>0
such that,

(0)

For each integer
i
, we have that,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\subseteq\bigcup_{|n|\leq A}\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
see
Definition

3.8
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
.

(1)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n+i\geq A
.

(3)

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}
.

We say
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable
if we further have,

(3)
′

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
and
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-i,A-i]}_{A}
.

Convention 4.4
.

We will use the following conventions to make notation less cumbersome.

(1)

We write approx (resp. quasiapprox) instead of approximable/approximability (resp. quasiapproximable/quasiapproximability).

(2)

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category. We say
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-approx) if there exists a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
) for
\mathsf{T}
, and
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-approx), see
Definition

4.1
. Similar convention holds for (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, see
Definition

4.2
.

(3)

Finally, we say
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-approx) if
\mathsf{T}
is a compactly generated triangulated category,
\mathcal{G}
is a generating sequence for
\mathsf{T}
,
\mathcal{R}
is an orthogonal metric on
\mathsf{T}
, and
A
is a positive integer satisfying the conditions of
\mathcal{G}
-approx (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-approx). Similar convention holds for (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox.

Remark 4.5
.

From
Definition

3.19
(1), we get that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}\preceq_{\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
as extended good metrics on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
, see
Definition

3.3
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Note that this holds for any weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx (or weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox) triangulated category .

We now prove some results related to the ideas introduced above. We begin by showing that we can get “arbitrarily good approximations” of objects in
\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
), an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(see
Definition

3.1
) and an integer
A>0
satisfying
Definition

4.1
(3). Then, with notation as in
Notation

3.6
,

(1)

Let
i<j
be any positive integers. For any
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-j+1,A-i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
. If we further assume that
\mathsf{T}
satisfies
Definition

4.1
(3^{\prime})
, we can produce the triangle with
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-j+1,A-i]}_{(j-i)A}
.

(2)

For any
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a sequence
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
of objects in
\mathsf{T}
with compatible maps to
F
, such that for all
n\geq 1
, in the triangle
E_{n}\to F\to D_{n}\to\Sigma E_{n}
, we have
E_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}
and
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
. If we further assume that
\mathsf{T}
satisfies
Definition

4.1
(3^{\prime})
, we can get the sequence with
E_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}_{nA}
for all
n\geq 1
.

Further, the analogous result for
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
holds if we instead assume that
\mathsf{T}
is a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
, an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
and an integer
A>0
satisfying
Definition

4.2
(3) (resp.
Definition

4.2
(3
′
)) instead of
Definition

4.1
(3) (resp.
Definition

4.1
(3
′
)).

Proof.

Clearly
(2)\implies(1)
. So, it is enough to show
(2)
.
We proceed by induction on
n\geq 1
. Let
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Then, we get the case
n=1
by
Definition

4.1
(3) and (3
′
). Now, suppose we have got a sequence
E_{1}\to\cdots\to E_{n}
with the required properties. So, we have the triangle
E_{n}\to F\to D_{n}\to\Sigma E_{n}
with
E_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}
and
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
. If
\mathsf{T}
further satisfies
Definition

4.1
(2^{\prime})
, we can assume that
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}_{nA}
.

By
Definition

4.1
(3), we know that there exists a triangle
E^{\prime}\to D_{n}\to D_{n+1}\to\Sigma E^{\prime}
with
E^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-n-i]}
and
D_{n+1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n+1}
. If
\mathsf{T}
further satisfies
Definition

4.1
(3^{\prime})
, we can arrange so that
E^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-n-i]}_{A}
.

Using the octahedral axiom on
F\to D_{n}\to D_{n+1}
, we get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n+1}}

Note that here

E_{n+1}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}\star\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-n-i]}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-i]}

If
\mathsf{T}
further satisfies
Definition

4.1
(2^{\prime})
, we get that

E_{n+1}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i+1,A-i]}_{nA}\star\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-n-i]}_{A}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-n-i,A-i]}_{(n+1)A}

and so, we are done via induction.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a pre-generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.5
) and an extended good metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
) such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for
i>>0
.
Then,

(1)

\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}_{i}=\{0\}

(2)

Let
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
be a sequence with compatible maps to an object
F
, such that for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\operatorname{Cone}(E_{i}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
for all
i>>0
. Then the non-canonical map
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{n}\to F
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1)

Let
F\in\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Then,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},F)=0
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by hypothesis. As
(\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{j})^{\perp}=0
, see
Definition

3.5
, we get that
F=0
.

(2)

Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
be any integer, and let
G\in\mathcal{G}^{j}
. Note that
\Sigma^{i}\mathcal{R}_{j+|i|}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{j}
for any integers
i
and
j
, see
Definition

3.1
(2). So, by the hypothesis,
there exists an integer
a>0
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,\mathcal{R}_{p})=0
for all
p\geq a
and
-1\leq r\leq 2
.

As
\operatorname{Cone}(E_{q}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{a}
for all
q>>0
, by the long exact sequence we get from applying the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,-)
to the triangle
\Sigma^{-1}\operatorname{Cone}(E_{q}\to F)\to E_{q}\to F\to\operatorname{Cone}(E_{q}\to F)
, we get that the natural map
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,E_{q})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,F)
is an isomorphism for all
q>>0
and
-1\leq r\leq 1
. Let
E\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{n}
. As
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,E)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,E_{n})
for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
by
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
, we get that the natural map gives an isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,E)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{r}G,F)
for
-1\leq r\leq 1
.

So, by the long exact sequence we get from applying the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,-)
to the triangle
E\to F\to\operatorname{Cone}(E\to F)\to E[1]
, we get that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,\operatorname{Cone}(E\to F))=0
. As
G\in\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{j}
was arbitrary and as
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{j}
generates
\mathsf{T}
(see
Definition

3.5
), we get that
\operatorname{Cone}(E\to F)\cong 0
. Therefore,
E\to F
is an isomorphism, and we are done.

∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx, see
Convention

4.4
. For any object
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
consider the sequence
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
with compatible maps to
F
we get from
Proposition

4.6
(2). Then,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{n}\cong F
. In particular,
F\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-i+1]}
, see
Notation

3.6
.

The analogous result holds for
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
if instead
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox.

Proof.

Let
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
be any object. Consider the sequence
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
we get from
Proposition

4.6
(2). We have that
\operatorname{Cone}(E_{n}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{n+i}
for all
n
by
Proposition

4.6
. So, by
Lemma

4.7
,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\cong F
. Note that
E_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-i]}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
again by
Proposition

4.6
(2). From the triangle,

\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{i}\to\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\to\Sigma\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{i}

we get that
F\cong\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-i+1]}
, see
Notation

3.6
.
∎

We now show that the orthogonal metric in the definition of approximability lies in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (see
Definition

3.15
). Further, we show that the extended good metric it induces on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
is equivalent to the metric
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. This is an important fact, as, for example, equivalent metrics give rise to equivalent categories via the completion machinery, as the Cauchy sequences remain the same, see
Definition

2.14
and
Definition

2.15
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, see
Convention

4.4
. Then,

(1)

The metrics
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
(
Definition

3.8
(2)) and
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
are
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent, see
Definition

3.3
for the definition of the equivalence relation.

(2)

\mathcal{R}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
is
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent to the metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
(see
Definition

3.8
) as metrics on
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. This implies that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
is
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent to the metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Further, if
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx (see
Definition

4.2
), then,

(3)

\mathcal{R}
lies in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics on
\mathsf{T}
, that is, it is equivalent to the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

(1)

We already know
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}\preceq_{\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
by
Remark

4.5
. For the other direction, we need to show that there exists a integer
B>0
such that
\mathcal{R}_{i+B}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. We claim that the integer
B
can be chosen to be
A+1
. Let
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for any
i\geq 0
. Then, by
Corollary

4.8
,
F\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}
(see
Notation

3.6
). And so,
F\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}=\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i]}
, see
Remark

3.7
. But, by definition,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i]}
. And so,
\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
, and we are done.

(2)

From
Definition

4.2
(1), we have that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. And so, for all
i\geq 0
,

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}=\ ^{\perp}\left((\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A})^{\perp}\right)\subseteq\ ^{\perp}\left(\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{i}

Conversely, we have that,

\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}\subseteq\ ^{\perp}\left(\big(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}\big)^{\perp}\right)=\ ^{\perp}\left(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-i]^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}

for all
i
where we get the first inclusion by
Corollary

4.8
,

(3)

From
Definition

4.1
(1), we have that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i
. And so, for all
i
,

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}=\ ^{\perp}\left((\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A})^{\perp}\right)\subseteq\ ^{\perp}\left(\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{i}

Conversely, by
Corollary

4.8
,
\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}
for all
i\geq 0
. And so,

\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1}=\ ^{\perp}\left((\mathcal{R}_{i+A+1})^{\perp}\right)\subseteq\ ^{\perp}\left(\big(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,-i]}\big)^{\perp}\right)=\ ^{\perp}\left(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-i]^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}

for all
i\geq 0
.

∎

We now give a converse to
Proposition

4.9
(3). That is, we show that we can check approximability with any orthogonal metric in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx triangulated category (see
Convention

4.4
), and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
be a metric in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics on
\mathsf{T}
(see
Definition

3.15
). Then, there exists an integer
A^{\prime}>0
such that
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx. If
\mathsf{T}
is further
\mathcal{G}
-approx, then we can choose the integer
A^{\prime}
such that
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is
\mathcal{G}
-approx.

The analogous result holds for (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-approx.

Proof.

As
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx, there exists an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
and an integer
A>0
satisfying
Remark

4.3
(0)-(3). By
Proposition

4.9
(2),
\mathcal{R}
lies in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics. As
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
also lies in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class, we get that
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent. So, there exists an integer
B>0
such that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i+B}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i+B}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. We now show that the conditions (0)-(3) of
Remark

4.3
hold with the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and the integer
A^{\prime}=A+B>0
. Note that
Remark

4.3
(0) holds trivially as it does not involve the orthogonal metric.

(1)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A+B}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i+B}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i})=0
for
n+i\geq A+B
, as
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i-B}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

Let
i\geq 0
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\subset\mathcal{R}_{i-B}
. By
Proposition

4.6
(1), there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-(i+B),A-i+B]}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+B+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

If
\mathsf{T}
is further
\mathcal{G}
-approx with the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
and integer
A>0
satisfying the conditions of
\mathcal{G}
-approx, then,

(3
′
)

Let
i\geq 0
, and
F\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\subset\mathcal{R}_{i-B}
. By
Proposition

4.6
(1), there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-(i+B),A-i+B]}_{(2B+1)A}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+B+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

And so we have shown
\mathcal{G}
-approx using the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and the integer
A^{\prime}=2AB+A>0
.
∎

The following can be proved analogously to
Proposition

4.10
.

Remark 4.11
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx triangulated category (see
Definition

4.1
), and let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be a generating sequence
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent to
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.12
) and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
be a metric in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics on
\mathsf{T}
(see
Definition

3.15
). Then, there exists an integer
A^{\prime}>0
such that
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is weakly
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-approx. If
\mathsf{T}
was
\mathcal{G}
-approx, we can further choose
A^{\prime}
such that
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-approx. The analogous result also holds for (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox.

We now record a lemma to be used later.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox. Then, there exists
b\geq 0
such that for any integer
i
and positive integer
j
,
\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{|n|\leq(j+2b)A}\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{R}_{i+j}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.9
(1), there exists an integer
b\geq 0
so that for all
i
,

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+b}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i-b}

see
Definition

3.8
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. By
Lemma

3.11
(1),

\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i-b}\subseteq\bigcup_{|n|\leq(j+2b)A}\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+b+j}\subseteq\bigcup_{|n|\leq(j+2b)A}\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{R}_{i+j}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}

which is what we needed to show.
∎

As might be clear from the name,
\mathcal{G}
-approximability is closely related to the notion of approximability introduced by Neeman, see
[
Nee21b
, Definition 0.21]
. In fact, approximability is a special case of
\mathcal{G}
-approximability. The connection is explored below.

Remark 4.13
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We define the finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{-n}G\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(see
Definition

3.8
). Note that by
Example

3.13
, if we choose a different compact generator we get a
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent generating sequence (
Definition

3.12
). Then, it is immediate from the definitions that,

(1)

There is a bijection between the preferred equivalence class of t-structures on
\mathsf{T}
, see
Definition

2.18
, and those orthogonal metrics
\mathcal{R}
in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
), such that
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathsf{U}
for a t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
. In particular, the t-structure generated by
G
(see
Theorem

2.5
) is exactly
\big(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}},(\Sigma\mathcal{R}_{0}^{G})^{\perp}\big)
(see
Definition

3.8
for the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
).

(2)

The closure of the compacts
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}=\mathsf{T}^{-}_{c}
. See
Definition

2.19
(2) for the definition of
\mathsf{T}^{-}_{c}
, and
Definition

3.17
for the definition of
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We define the finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{-n}G\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(
Definition

3.8
). Then,

(i)

\mathsf{T}
is a weakly approximable triangulated category (
Definition

2.20
) if and only if
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
).

(ii)

\mathsf{T}
is an approximable triangulated category (
Definition

2.20
) if and only if
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
).

Proof.

Both (i) and (ii) follow the same way, so we only prove (i). Suppose
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable. Then, as mentioned in
Remark

4.13
(1), the t-structure compactly generated by
G
gives
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
, which is a metric in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics, under the bijection given in
Remark

4.13
(1). By
Proposition

4.10
,
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
G
-approximable with respect to any orthogonal metric in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics, in particular with
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
. It is easy to see that this gives us that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly approximable.
Remark

4.3
(1), (2), and (3) imply
Definition

2.20
(1), (2), and (3) respectively with the t-structure generated by
G
, see
Theorem

2.5
.

Conversely, suppose
\mathsf{T}
is weakly approximable. So, there exists a t-structure
(\mathsf{T}^{\leq 0},\mathsf{T}^{\geq 0})
, and an integer
A>0
such that the conditions (1)-(3) of
Definition

2.20
are satisfied with the compact generator
G
. We will show that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable by showing the conditions (0)-(3) of
Remark

4.3
hold with the orthogonal good metric
\mathcal{R}_{i}=\Sigma^{i}\mathsf{T}^{\leq 0}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and the integer
A>0
.

(0)

As
\mathcal{G}^{n}=\{\Sigma^{-n}G\}
, we get that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i]}=\langle G\rangle^{(-\infty,-i]}
by definition, see
Definition

3.8
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Also see,
Notation

3.6
and
Notation

2.2
.

(1)

As
\Sigma^{A}G\in\mathsf{T}^{\leq 0}=\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}=\langle G\rangle^{(-\infty,-i-A]}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

(2)

As
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{-i}G,\mathsf{T}^{\leq 0})=0
for all
i\geq A
, we get that,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{-n}G,\mathsf{T}^{\leq-i})=0

for all
n+i\geq A
.

(3)

For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathsf{T}^{\leq-i}
, we get that
\Sigma^{-i}F\in\mathsf{T}^{\leq 0}
. So, by the
Definition

2.20
(3), we get a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\langle G\rangle^{[-A-i,A-i]}
and
D\in\mathsf{T}^{\leq-i-1}=\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
, which is what we needed as
\langle G\rangle^{[-A-i,A-i]}=\mathcal{G}^{[-A-i,A-i]}
.

∎

5.
The closure of the compacts

We now give some results related to the subcategory
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, see
Definition

3.17
. Whenever there is an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
given on
\mathsf{T}
,
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
will always mean the closure of the compacts with respect to the metric
\mathcal{R}
unless explicitly stated otherwise. For a triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
), the closure of the compacts will be with respect to the metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
unless explicitly stated otherwise

Now, we prove that under the assumption of (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
Definition

4.2
), we can approximate an object in the closure of compact by a “very nice” Cauchy sequence, see
Corollary

5.4
. This will be used to prove the representability results in
Section

6
. We begin with a few technical lemmas.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx, see
Convention

4.4
. Then,
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

m>0
,
and any
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists an object
L
and a triangle
E\to K\oplus L\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,A-m-i]}
, see
Notation

3.6
. If
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is further
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or
\mathcal{G}
-approx, then we can choose the triangle so that
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}_{mA}
.

Proof.

Let
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
for some integer
i
. For every
m>0
, there exists a triangle

(5.1)

E_{m}\to K\to D_{m}\to\Sigma E_{m}\quad\text{with }E_{m}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}\text{ and }D_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+m}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}

by
Proposition

4.6
(1). If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}-
quasiapprox, we can further get a triangle with
E_{m}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}_{mA}
.

As
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, there exists an integer
B>0
, such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
by
Lemma

3.21
. By increasing
B
if necessary, we may assume that
B\geq m+2A+i
so that we have
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma E_{m},\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
, as
E_{m}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}_{mA}
and by
Definition

4.2
(2) we get that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]},\mathcal{R}_{m+2A-1+i})=0
. Hence, we get that,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(D_{m},\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
from the triangle
5.1
.

By
Proposition

4.6
(1), we can construct a triangle for
D_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{m+i}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
as follows,

(5.2)

E^{\prime}\to D_{m}\to Q\to\Sigma E^{\prime}\quad\text{with }E^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-B+1,A-m-i]}\text{ and }Q\in\mathcal{R}_{B}\cap\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}\,.

As
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(D_{m},\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
, the map
D_{m}\to Q
is zero, and so
D_{m}
is a summand of
E^{\prime}
by
5.2
. Hence
D_{m}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-B+1,A-m-i]}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-m-i]}
.

So,
K\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}\star\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-m-i]}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we further have that
K\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-A-m+1-i,A-i]}_{mA}\star\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{(-\infty,A-m-i]}
.
We define the following full subcategories,

•

\mathcal{X}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Coprod}(\mathcal{G}[-A-m+1-i,A-i])

•

\mathcal{X}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Coprod}_{mA}(\mathcal{G}[-A-m+1-i,A-i])

•

\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Coprod}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,A-m-i])

see
Notation

2.2
.
We have that
K\in\operatorname{smd}(\mathcal{X}_{i})\star\operatorname{smd}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\operatorname{smd}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\star\mathcal{Z})
with
i=1
or
2
depending on
\mathsf{T}
being weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
i=1
), or
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
i=2
). So, there exists an object
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}\star\mathcal{Z}
with maps
K\xrightarrow{f}K^{\prime}\to K
composing to identity.

We now define the corresponding small subcategories,

•

\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{coprod}(\mathcal{G}[-A-m+1-i,A-i])

•

\mathcal{A}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{coprod}_{mA}(\mathcal{G}[-A-m+1-i,A-i])

•

\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{coprod}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,A-m-i])

By
[
Nee21a
, Lemma 1.8]
, any map from compacts to the big categories
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}
and
\mathcal{Z}
factors through the corresponding small category
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
. Observe that
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
and so the conditions of
[
Nee21a
, Remark 1.7]
are satisfied. Hence, we can apply
[
Nee21a
, Lemma 1.6]
to the map
K\xrightarrow{f}K^{\prime}
, as it is a map from a compact object
K
to
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}\star\mathcal{Z}
, to get that it must factor through
K^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\star\mathcal{C}
. The composite
K\to K^{\prime\prime}\to K^{\prime}\to K
is thus the identity morphism, which gives us that
K
is a summand of
K^{\prime\prime}
. And so,
K\in\operatorname{smd}(\mathcal{A}_{i}\star\mathcal{C})
which is what we needed to show.

∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx, see
Convention

4.4
. Then, there exists an integer
B>0
, such that for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E\to K\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
.

If
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is further
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or
\mathcal{G}
-approx, then we can further arrange
B
so that we have
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}_{B}
.

Proof.

Let
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We apply
Lemma

5.1
to
K
with
m=nA+1
for any integer
n>1
, to get triangles
E\to K\oplus L\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+1)A-i,A-i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-(n-1)A-1-i]}
(see
Notation

3.6
). If the category is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we can further assume that
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+1)A-i,A-i]}_{(nA+1)A}
.

Note that
D\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{(n-2)A+1+i}
as
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
by
Remark

4.3
(1), see
Definition

3.8
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Applying
Lemma

5.1
to
D
with
m=6A
, we can get a triangle,
E^{\prime}\to D\oplus M\to D^{\prime}\to\Sigma E^{\prime}
with
E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,-(n-3)A-1-i]}
and
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-(n+3)A-1-i]}
. We can further assume that
E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,-(n-3)A-1-i]}_{6A^{2}}
if
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox.

Now, we apply the octahedral axiom to
K\oplus L\oplus M\to D\oplus M\to D^{\prime}
, where the map
K\oplus L\oplus M\to D\oplus M
is the sum of the map
K\oplus L\to D
and the identity map on
M
, to get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus L\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}

This gives us that
E^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+1)A-i,A-i]}\star\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,-(n-3)A-1-i]}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,A-i]}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, then
E^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,A-i]}_{(n+6)A^{2}+A}
.

D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-(n+3)A-1-i]}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{(n-2)A+1+i}
and
E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+5)A-i,-(n-3)A-1-i]}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{(n-4)A+1+i}
, again as
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+A}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
by
Definition

4.1
(1). And hence by the triangle
E^{\prime}\to D\oplus M\to D^{\prime}\to\Sigma E^{\prime}
, we get that
M\in\mathcal{R}_{(n-4)A+1+i}
using the fact that
\mathcal{R}_{(n-4)A+1+i}
is closed under summands as
\mathcal{R}
is an orthogonal metric.

Consider the diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus L\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}

where the vertical map is the direct sum of the identity on
L
and the zero map. The map from the top left to the bottom right in this diagram lies in

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{[-(n+1)A-i,A-i]},\mathcal{R}_{(n+2)A+1+i})=0

by
Definition

4.2
(2) as
(n+2)A+1+i-(n+1)A-i=A+1
.

So, we can complete to the following
3\times 3
diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus L\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma K\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime\prime}}}

Note that
\widetilde{E}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n-5)A-i,A-i+1]}
and
D^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{(n-2)A+i}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we have that
\widetilde{E}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-(n-5)A-i,A-i+1]}_{(2n+6)A^{2}+2A}
. Applying the octahedral axiom to the composable morphisms
\widetilde{E}\to K\oplus\Sigma K\oplus M\to K\oplus\Sigma K
, where the second map is just the projection, we get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma K\oplus M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma M}}

which gives us the triangle
\widetilde{E}\to K\oplus\Sigma K\to\widetilde{D}\to\Sigma\widetilde{E}
with
\widetilde{D}\in\mathcal{R}_{(n-4)A+i}
.

So the upshot of all of the above computation is that given any positive integer
n
, there exists an integer
B^{1}_{n}
such that for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E_{n}\to K\oplus\Sigma K\to D_{n}\to\Sigma E_{n}
with
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{1}_{n}-i,B^{1}_{n}-i]}
and
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we can further arrange that
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{1}_{n}-i,B^{1}_{n}-i]}_{B^{1}_{n}}
.

Now, we will inductively show that given any positive integer
n
and any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists an integer
B^{j}_{n}
such that for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, there exists a triangle
E_{n}\to K\oplus\Sigma^{2j-1}K\to D_{n}\to\Sigma E_{n}
with
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{j}_{n}-i,B^{j}_{n}-i]}
and
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we can arrange so that
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{j}_{n}-i,B^{j}_{n}-i]}_{B^{j}_{n}}
.

We prove the above claim for
j\geq 1
, and
\mathsf{T}
weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox. The other cases are analogous. We have already proven the result for
j=1
, so, we just need to prove the inductive step now. So, assume that we have the result up to some integer
j\geq 1
. Let
i
be any integer,
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
any object, and
n
any positive integer. By the induction hypothesis, we have a triangle
E\to K\oplus\Sigma^{2j-1}K\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{j}_{n+3}-i,B^{j}_{n+3}-i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n+3}
. Choose an
l\geq n+3
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{j}_{n+3}-i,B^{j}_{n+3}-i]},\mathcal{R}_{p+i})=0
for all
p\geq l-1
which exists by
Definition

4.2
(2).

By the
j=1
case, we have a triangle
\widetilde{E}\to K\oplus\Sigma K\to\widetilde{D}\to\Sigma\widetilde{E}
such that
\widetilde{E}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{1}_{l}-i,B^{1}_{l}-i]}
and
\widetilde{D}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+l}
.

So, we get the following diagram with the vertical map given by the identity map on
K
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma^{2j-1}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma^{-1}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{D}}

As the map from the top left to the bottom right vanishes, we get a
3\times 3
diagram as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma^{2j-1}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma^{-1}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}K\oplus\Sigma^{2j}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}D_{n}}}

The shift of the bottom triangle is what we were after, which proves the claim by induction.

Now we prove the statement of the lemma. By
Lemma

4.12
, there exists an integer
r
such that
\Sigma^{2r}K\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. We can do this as follows. By
Lemma

4.12
, there exists an integer
a
with
|a|\leq(2+2b)A
such that
\Sigma^{a}K\in\mathcal{R}_{i+2}
. Therefore
\Sigma^{a}K,\Sigma^{a+1}K\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. So we can choose
2r
to be the even integer in the set
\{a,a+1\}
.

Note that
|r|\leq(1+b)A
independent of
K
and
i
, where
b
is as in
Lemma

4.12
. From the above claim, we get a triangle
E\to K\oplus\Sigma^{2r-1}K\to D^{\prime}\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{r}_{1}-i,B^{r}_{1}-i]}
and
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable, we can further arrange that
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B^{r}_{1}-i,B^{r}_{1}-i]}_{B^{r}_{1}}
. We apply the octahedral axiom to
E\to K\oplus\Sigma^{2r-1}K\to K
with second map being the obvious projection map, to get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\oplus\Sigma^{2r-1}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{2r}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{2r}K}}

E\to K\to D\to\Sigma E
is the required triangle. So, we have proven the result with
B=\operatorname{max}\{B_{1}^{r}:|r|\leq(1+b)A\}
, with
b
as in
Lemma

4.12
. Note that this
B
is independent of the integer
i
and the object
K\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approx, see
Convention

4.4
. Choose an integer
B
as in
Lemma

5.2
. Then, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
(
Definition

3.17
), there exists a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}
(see
Notation

3.6
) and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. If
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is further assumed to be
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or
\mathcal{G}
-approx, then we can further get the triangle with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}_{B}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
. By the definition of
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, see
Definition

3.17
, we can get a triangle
K\to F\to D_{1}\to\Sigma K
such that
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. The triangle then gives us that
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, as
\Sigma^{-1}D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
(
Definition

3.1
(2)), and
K\in\Sigma^{-1}D_{1}\star F
(
Definition

3.1
(1)). So, we can apply
Lemma

5.2
to
K
, to get a triangle
E\to K\to D_{2}\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}
and
D_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. If
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, we can further assume that
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-i,B-i]}_{B}
.

We then complete the composable morphisms
E\to K\to F
to the octahedron,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}

As
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
both lie in
\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
, so does
D
, and hence
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
is the required triangle and we are done.

∎

Note that
Proposition

5.3
shows that
\mathcal{G}
-approx (resp. weak
\mathcal{G}
-approx) implies
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (resp. weak
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox).

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
be weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox. Then, for any object
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
(
Definition

3.17
), there exists a sequence of compact objects
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
mapping to
F
such that
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i+1,B-i]}
and
D_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Cone}(E_{n}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
for all
n\geq 1
, see
Notation

3.6
.

If
(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{R},A)
is further assumed to be
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, then we can further get a sequence such that
E_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i]}_{nB}
for all
n\geq 1
.

In either case, the non-canonical map
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E}_{n}\to F
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We prove this statement inductively on
n
. We get the case
n=1
by
Proposition

5.3
. Now, suppose we have the required sequence up to some integer
n\geq 1
. In particular, we have a triangle
E_{n}\to F\to D_{n}\to\Sigma E_{n}
with
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
and
E_{n}
in
\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i+1,B-i]}
or
\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i+1,B-i]}_{nB}
depending on if
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox respectively.

We apply
Proposition

5.3
to
D_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n}
to get the triangle
\widetilde{E}\to D_{n}\to D_{n+1}\to\Sigma\widetilde{E}
with
D_{n+1}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+n+1}
and
E_{n}
in
\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i-n]}
or
\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i-n]}_{B}
depending on if
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox or
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox respectively. Applying the octahedral axiom to
F\to D_{n}\to D_{n+1}
, we get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{n+1}}

And so, we get the required extension of the sequence as,

E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i+1,B-i]}\star\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i-n]}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i]}

if
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox. Further, if
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox,

E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i+1,B-i]}_{nB}\star\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i-n]}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{[-B-n-i,B-i]}_{(n+1)B}

Finally,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\cong F
by
Lemma

4.7
(2).
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Note that in
Proposition

5.3
and
Corollary

5.4
, we can choose
\mathcal{R}
to be any orthogonal metric in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
) by
Proposition

4.10
.

6.
Representability results

In this section, we will prove the main representability results. We start with the following definition inspired by
[
Nee21b
, Definition 7.3]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.5
), and an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
) in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence of orthogonal metrics, see
Definition

3.15
. Then, for
n
a positive integer or
\infty
, we define a
strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system
to be a sequence
E_{0}\to E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to\cdots
such that each
E_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,\infty)}_{n}
(
Notation

3.6
) and for all
i\geq 0
,
\operatorname{Cone}(E_{i}\to E_{i+1})\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Let
F\in\mathsf{T}
and suppose we are given a map from a sequence
E_{*}=\{E_{i}\to E_{i+1}\}_{i\geq 0}
in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
to
F
. Then, the sequence is a
strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for
F

if,

(1)

The natural map
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(-,E_{i})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(-,F)
is an isomorphism on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Cone}(E_{n}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
.

We also call a strong
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}
-approximating system a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system as we have
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
a triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
), and an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
) on
\mathsf{T}
such that for all
n
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
i>>0
. We consider the closure of the compacts
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
(
Definition

3.17
) with respect to the metric
\mathcal{R}
. Then, for
n\in[1,\infty]
, that is, for
n
either a positive integer or
\infty
,

(1)

Any strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system (
Definition

6.1
) is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for
F=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E_{i}}
, and further,
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
.

(2)

Given
F\in

\mathsf{T}^{c}
and a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system
E_{*}
for
F
(
Definition

6.1
), the non-canonical map
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\to F
is an isomorphism.

(3)

Any object
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
has a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system, see
Definition

6.1
.

Proof.

First we prove (1). Let
E_{*}
be a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system, and let
F=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E_{i}}
. For each
i,j\geq 1
, we have triangles
E_{j}\to E_{j+i}\to C_{i,j}\to\Sigma E_{j}
. We will prove that
C_{i,j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j+1}
for all
i,j\geq 1
by induction on
i
. The base case
i=1
follows from the definition, see
Definition

6.1
. Suppose we know the result up to some positive integer
i
. In particular, we have triangles
E_{j}\to E_{j+i}\to C_{i,j}\to\Sigma E_{j}
with
C_{i,j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j+1}
for all
j\geq 1
. Again, as
E_{*}
is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system, we get triangles
E_{j+i}\to E_{j+i+1}\to D_{j}\to\Sigma E_{j+i}
with
D_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j+i+1}
for all
j\geq 1
. Applying the octahedral axiom to
E_{j}\to E_{j+i}\to E_{j+i+1}
, we get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{j+i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{j+i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{(i+1,j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j}}

The triangle forming the middle row gives us the induction step as, from the triangle forming the rightmost column we have that,

C_{(i+1,j)}\in C_{i,j}\star D_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{j+1}\star\mathcal{R}_{j+i+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{j+1}\star\mathcal{R}_{j+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{j+1}

for all
j\geq 1
, see
Definition

3.1
.

Consider the following 3
\times
3 diagram which we construct from the top left commutative square in which the vertical maps are the direct sums of the obvious map
E_{j}\to E_{j+i}
for all
i\geq 0
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}E_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}E_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}E_{j+i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}E_{j+i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}C_{i,j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}C_{i,j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\text{shift}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\text{shift}}

As
C_{i,j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j+1}
for all
i\geq 0
and
\mathcal{R}_{j+1}
is closed under coproducts as it is an orthogonal metric, we get that
C_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
(see
Definition

3.1
). By the triangle forming the rightmost column of this diagram,
C_{j}=\operatorname{Cone}(E_{j}\to F)\in\mathcal{R}_{j}
for all
j\geq 1
. Further, for any compact object
E
,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E,E_{i})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E,F)
by
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
. And so,
E_{*}
is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for
F
. The rightmost column also shows that
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

For the proof of (2), let
E=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E_{i}}
. Complete the natural map
E\to F
to a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
. We apply the octahedral axiom to
E_{i}\to E\to F
for any
i\geq 0
, to get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

By the proof of part (1), we know that
C_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and hence
\Sigma C_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i-1}
, see
Definition

3.1
(2). As
E_{*}
is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for
F
, we get that
C^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i-1}
. Consider the triangle
C_{i}^{\prime}\to D\to\Sigma C_{i}\to\Sigma C_{i}^{\prime}
, we get that
D\in C_{i}^{\prime}\star\Sigma C_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i-1}\star\mathcal{R}_{i-1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i-1}
by
Definition

3.1
(1).

As this is true for any
i>0
,
D\in\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{R}_{n}=0
by
Lemma

4.7
. So,
E=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E_{i}}\to F
is an isomorphism, which proves (1).

Finally, for (3), we proceed inductively. By the definition of
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, see
Definition

3.17
, there exists a triangle
E_{1}\to F\to D_{1}\to\Sigma E_{1}
such that
E_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Now suppose we have constructed the sequence up to a positive integer
i
. We
choose an integer
N>0
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E_{i},\mathcal{R}_{n})=0
for all
n\geq N
which exists by
Lemma

3.21
. Finally, again by the definition of the closure of the compacts (
Definition

3.17
), we choose a triangle
E_{i+1}\to F\to D_{i+1}\to\Sigma E_{i+1}
such that
E_{i+1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{i+1}\in\mathcal{R}_{N+i+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. By the choice of
N
, we get that the composite
(E_{i}\to F\to D_{i+1})=0
, and hence
E_{i}\to F
factors through
E_{i+1}
, giving us the required sequence by induction, proving (3).

∎

Other than
Section

6.1
, throughout the rest of this section, we will be working in the following setup.

Setup 6.3
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be
R
-linear triangulated category, with
R
a commutative noetherian ring. Further, assume
\mathsf{T}
has a finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
), an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
) in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics on
\mathsf{T}
(
Definition

3.15
), and a positive integer
A>0
such that,

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G_{1},G_{2})
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
G_{1},G_{2}\in\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n+i\geq A
.

As
\mathcal{G}
is a finite generating sequence,
\mathcal{G}^{i}=\{G^{i}_{j}:j\in J_{i}\}
for finite sets
J_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In what follows, we will consider the closure of the compacts
\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) corresponding to the metric
\mathcal{R}
.

Remark 6.4
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
.
Recall that the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
(see
Definition

3.8
) also lies in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics. Hence, there exists an integer
l
such that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+l}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, see
Definition

3.3
(2). But, by the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
, we have that
\mathcal{G}^{-i}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i-l}
, see
Definition

3.8
. And so,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{G}^{-i})=0
for all
n+i\geq A+l
.

We remind the reader of the following standard definition.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
\mathsf{S}
be a full and replete subcategory of a
R
-linear triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
with
\Sigma\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{S}
. A functor
H:\mathsf{S}^{\text{op}}\to\operatorname{Mod}(R)
is called a
\mathsf{S}
-cohomological functor if it takes triangles to long exact sequences of
R
-modules. Equivalently, we get an exact sequence
H(Z)\to H(Y)\to H(X)
in
\operatorname{Mod}(R)
for any triangle
X\to Y\to Z\to\Sigma X
in
\mathsf{T}
with
X,Y
and
Z
in
\mathsf{S}
.

We will be mostly interested in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functors. So, we define a few special classes of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functors now.

Definition 6.6
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
, and let
n\in[1,\infty]
. Then, we say a
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functor
H
is,

(1)

\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
if
H(F)
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
F\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, and
H(\mathcal{G}^{i})=0
for all
i>>0
.

(2)

\mathcal{G}
-finite
if
H(F)
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
F\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, and
H(\mathcal{G}^{i})=0
for all
|i|>>0
.

see
Notation

3.6
for the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

The following example shows what the
\mathcal{G}
-finite and
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functors look like for some specific cases if interest.

Example 6.7
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
. Further assume that
\mathsf{T}
has a single compact generator
G
. Recall that we had a finite generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
)
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathsf{T}
. Then,

(1)

If
\mathcal{G}^{n}=\{\Sigma^{-n}G\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then a
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor
H
is
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-finite) if for all objects
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
,
H(F)
is a finitely generated
R
-module and
H(\Sigma^{-i}F)=0
for all
i>>0
(resp.
|i|>>0|
). Note that this is the generating sequence used for approximability, see
Theorem

4.14
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{G}^{n}=\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then a
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor
H
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite (resp.
\mathcal{G}
-finite) if for all objects
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
,
H(F)
is a finitely generated
R
-module and
H(\Sigma^{i}F)=0
for all
i>>0
(resp.
|i|>>0|
).

These follow from the
Definition

6.6
and the observation that for any
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, there exists
n\geq 0
such that
F\in\langle G\rangle^{[-n,n]}
as
G
is a compact generator of
\mathsf{T}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

Notation 6.8
.

For any
F\in\mathsf{T}
, we have a
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.5
)
\mathcal{Y}(F):[\mathsf{T}^{c}]^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Mod}(R)
which is the restriction of the Yoneda functor. That is, for any
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we have
\mathcal{Y}(F)(K)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,F)
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
. Then, for any
F
in the closure of the compacts
\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
), we have that
\mathcal{Y}(F):[\mathsf{T}^{c}]^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Mod}(R)
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor, see
Definition

6.6
(2).

Proof.

Let,

\mathsf{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{H\in\mathsf{T}^{c}:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,H)\text{ is a finitely generated $R$-module for all }G\in\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}\right\}

Then,
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}\subseteq\mathsf{L}
by
Setup

6.3
(1). As
\mathsf{L}
is clearly closed under extensions and direct summands, and contains
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
, we get that
\operatorname{smd}(\operatorname{coprod}(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}))=\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\mathsf{L}
, see
Definition

3.8
(1). And so,
\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
.
Now, for any
L\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we define,

\mathsf{K}(L)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,L)\text{ is a finitely generated $R$-module}\}

\mathsf{K}(L)
is clearly closed under extensions and direct summands, and contains
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
by the paragraph above, and hence,
\mathsf{K}(L)=\mathsf{T}^{c}
. And so,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,L)
is a finitely generated module for all
K,L\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Now, let
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. Then, by the definition of
\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
), there exists a triangle
E_{1}\to F\to D_{1}\to\Sigma E_{1}
such that,
E_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. There exists
n>0
such that
E_{1}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-n,n]}
(see
Notation

3.6
) as
\mathcal{G}
is a generating sequence, see
Definition

3.8
(1). By
Remark

6.4
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{i},\mathcal{G}^{[-n,n]})=0
for all
i>>0
, which implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{i},E_{1})=0
for all
i>>0
. Also, by
Setup

6.3
(2),
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{i},\mathcal{R}_{1})=0
for all
i>>0
. And so,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{i},F)=0
for all
i>>0
. So, all that remains to show is that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,F)
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. Then, by
Lemma

3.21
, there exists an integer
j
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
i\geq j
. As
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, there exists a triangle
L\to F\to D\to\Sigma L
with
L\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}_{j+1}
. Consider the long exact sequence we get from applying the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,-)
to this triangle,

0=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,\Sigma^{-1}D)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,L)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,F)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,D)=0

This gives us that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,F)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,L)
, which is a finitely generated
R
-module, as we have shown above that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,L)
is a finitely generated
R
-module for any pair of compact objects
K
and
L
. Combined with the previous paragraph, we get that
\mathcal{Y}(F)
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor
∎

6.1.
Some important technical tools

We state a few important definitions and results from
[
Nee21b
]
, some of them suitably modified to this context, which we will be using later in this section. Note that we will not be in
Setup

6.3
in general in this subsection.

Definition 6.10
.

[
Nee21b
, Definition 5.1]
Let
\mathsf{T}
be a
R
-linear triangulated category, where
R
is a commutative ring. Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be full subcategories of
\mathsf{T}
, and
H
be a
\mathsf{B}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.5
). An
\mathsf{A}
-approximating system for
H
is a sequence
E_{1}\to E_{2}\to E_{3}\to E_{4}\to\cdots
in
\mathsf{T}
with a cofinal subsequence in
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}
such that there exists an isomorphism of functors
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(-,E_{i})|_{\mathsf{B}}\cong H(-)
.

Remark 6.11
.

Note that for any
n\in[1,\infty]
, any strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for an object
F
(
Definition

6.1
) gives a
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system of
\mathcal{Y}(F)
, as
F\cong\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}
by
Lemma

6.2
(2), which implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,F)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(K,E_{i})
for any
K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
by
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Definition 6.12
.

[
Nee21b
, Definition 6.2]
Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a triangulated subcategory
\mathsf{S}
. For any
\mathsf{S}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.5
)
H
, we define another
\mathsf{S}
-cohomological functor
\Sigma H
by
\Sigma H(s)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H(\Sigma^{-1}s)
for all
s\in\mathsf{S}
.

A
weak triangle
in the category of
\mathsf{S}
-cohomological functors
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{S}^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Mod}(R))
is a sequence
A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}\Sigma A
such that the following is true for any rotation of the sequence
A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{v}C\xrightarrow{w}\Sigma A
: For any triangle
x\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}y\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}z\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\Sigma x
in
\mathsf{T}
, and a commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(u^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

we can extend to a commutative diagram as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(u^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(v^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(w^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

Note that a weak triangle is exact by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.4]
.
A sequence
x\to y\to z\to\Sigma x
in
\mathsf{T}
is called a weak triangle if
\mathcal{Y}(-)
takes it to a weak triangle in
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathsf{S}^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Mod}(R))
.

Remark 6.13
.

We will be using
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
later in this section. In this remark, we adapt it to
Setup

6.3
. So, assume that we are in
Setup

6.3
. Further, suppose we are given

(i)

a morphism
\hat{\alpha}:\hat{A}\to\hat{B}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
).

(ii)

and
n,n^{\prime}\in[0,\infty]
, as well as a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n^{\prime}}
-approximating system
\mathfrak{U}_{*}
for
\hat{A}
and a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system
\mathfrak{B}_{*}
for
\hat{B}
, see
Definition

6.1
.

Then up to passing to a subsequence of
\mathfrak{B}_{*}
, by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 5.5]
, we have a map of sequences
\alpha_{*}:\mathfrak{U}_{*}\to\mathfrak{B}_{*}
compatible with
\hat{\alpha}
. As in
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
, we extend the map of sequences to triangles, to get, for each
n>0
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{U}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma\mathfrak{U}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{U}_{m+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{B}_{m+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}_{m+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma\mathfrak{U}_{m+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{X}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Y}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Z}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma\mathfrak{X}_{m}}}

By the definition of a strong approximating system, see
Definition

6.1
, we have that
\mathfrak{Y}_{m},\mathfrak{X}_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{m+1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{m}
. So,
\Sigma\mathfrak{X}_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{m}
by
Definition

3.1
(2). Hence, by
Definition

3.1
(1) we get that
\mathfrak{Z}_{m}\in\mathfrak{Y}_{m}\star\Sigma\mathfrak{X}_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{m}\star\mathcal{R}_{m}=\mathcal{R}_{m}
. And so, if we define
\mathfrak{C^{\prime}}
by
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{C}_{m+1}
, then, it is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n+n^{\prime}}
-approximating system. By
[
Nee21b
, Remark 6.3]
and
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
, the homotopy colimit of the sequence of triangles
\mathfrak{U}_{*}\to\mathfrak{B}_{*}\to\mathfrak{C}_{*}\to\Sigma\mathfrak{U}_{*}
gives us a weak triangle (
Definition

6.12
)
\hat{A}\to\hat{B}\to\hat{C}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}\mathfrak{C}_{i}\to\Sigma\hat{A}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

6.2.
The results

We prove the main representability theorems in this subsection. The main results are
Theorem

6.22
and
Theorem

6.24
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
. Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
(2)).
Then, we can produce a sequence
F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{n}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
with compatible maps,
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to H
such that,

(1)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
,
F_{i}
has a strong
\mathcal{G}_{i}
-approximating system (
Definition

6.1
), and the restricted map
\phi_{i}|_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}
is an epimorphism.

(2)

ker(
\phi_{i}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}})\subseteq
ker
\big(\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{i+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\big)
, that is, the map
\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{i+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
factors as
\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{i}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}H|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{i+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
for all
1\leq i<n

In particular, there exists an object
F\in\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
with a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system, and an epimorphism
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to H
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the integer
n\geq 1
. For
n=1
, we prove a stronger statement, which helps in proving the induction step.

(3)

Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
). Then, there exists an object
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) and an epimorphism
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H
, with
F
having a strong
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-approximating system
E_{*}
(
Definition

6.1
) such that each map
E_{n}\to E_{n+1}
is a split monomorphism.

We now prove (3). Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-cohomological functor. By the definition of a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite functor,
H(G)
is a finite
R
-module for all
G\in\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
, and is zero for
G\in\mathcal{G}^{n}
for
n>>0
. For each non-zero module
H(G^{i}_{j})
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
j\in J_{i}
, choose a finite set of generators
\{f_{ijk}:k\in K^{i}_{j}\}
, see
Setup

6.3
for the notation. By Yoneda lemma, each such element corresponds to a map,
\phi_{ijk}:\mathcal{Y}(G^{i}_{j})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H
.

Let
F=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{j\in J_{i}}\bigoplus_{k\in K^{i}_{j}}G^{i}_{j}
. Then we have the obvious epimorphism
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H
, given by,

\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{j\in J_{i}}\bigoplus_{k\in K^{i}_{j}}\mathcal{Y}(G^{i}_{j})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{(\phi_{ijk})}H

Now, we need to show the existence of a strong approximating system for
F
with the required properties. As
\mathcal{R}
is in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
), by
Remark

3.16
, there exists an integer
B>0
such that
\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i-B]}=\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+B}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
, see
Notation

3.6
and
Definition

3.8
(2). We define
E_{i}=\bigoplus_{n\geq-i-B+1}\bigoplus_{j\in J_{n}}\bigoplus_{k\in K^{n}_{j}}G^{n}_{j}
. This is a finite direct sum by the construction of
F
, as
H
is
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite, and so
E_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The canonical map
E_{i}\to E_{i+1}
is clearly a split monomorphism. Further, the cone of the canonical map
E_{i}\to F
lies in
\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i-B]}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and hence
E_{*}
is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-approximating system for
F
. So, we have proved (3).

Now, for the induction step. Suppose we know the result for all positive integers up to
n\geq 1
. Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{n+1}
-cohomological functor. Then
H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functor, and so, by induction hypothesis, there exists a sequence
F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{n}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
, with compatible epimorphisms
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
. In particular, we have the epimorphism
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
. Completing to a short exact sequence, we get,
0\to H^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{n}}H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to 0
. It is clear that
H^{\prime}
is also a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-cohomological functor. Now, we can use (3) to get an object
F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
with a strong
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-approximating system
E^{\prime}_{*}
with the maps being split monomorphisms, and an epimorphism
\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
.

We have the maps
\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
. As
F^{\prime}
admits a
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-approximating system (as any strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system gives a
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system as in
Definition

6.10
), by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 5.8]
, this composite is given by
\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
for a morphism
\alpha_{n}:F^{\prime}\to F_{n}
.

Now, we can apply
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
using
Remark

6.13
to get that,

(i)

There exists a weak triangle (
Definition

6.12
)
