F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}F_{n}\xrightarrow{\beta_{n}}F_{n+1}\to\Sigma F^{\prime}
in the category
\mathsf{T}^{c}
, and
F_{n+1}
admits a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n+1}
-approximating system.

(ii)

There exists a map
\phi_{n+1}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n+1})\to H
such that,
\phi_{n}
is equal to the composite

\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{n+1}|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}}H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}

As
\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
is an epimorphism, we have that,

\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{n}|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}H

is exact from the short exact sequence
0\to H^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{n}}H\to 0
. By (ii), we further get that

\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}

is also exact by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.4]
, which gives us
(2)
.

To prove (1), it just remains to show that
\phi_{n+1}
is an epimorphism. We note that we are in the setting of
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.6]
, with
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{G}_{1}
,
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{G}_{n}
, and the weak triangle
F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}F_{n}\xrightarrow{\beta_{n}}F_{n+1}\to\Sigma F^{\prime}
. So, by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.6]
, we get that
\phi_{n+1}
is an epimorphism.

∎

Remark 6.15
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
. As the proof of the
Lemma

6.14
is using induction, if we are given
j<i
, and a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathcal{G}_{i}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
)
H
with a sequence
F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{j}
satisfying
Lemma

6.14
for
H|_{\mathcal{G}_{j}}
, we can extend the sequence to
F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{i}
which satisfies
Lemma

6.14
for
H
. So, if we are given a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor
H
, we get an infinite sequence
F_{1}\to F_{2}\to F_{3}\to\cdots
, such that for any
i>0
,
F_{1}\to\cdots\to F_{i}
satisfy
Lemma

6.14
for
H|_{\mathcal{G}_{i}}
. As
F_{n}
has a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system, by
[
Nee21b
, Corollary 5.4]
we get that the morphisms
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
lifts uniquely to
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
. Further, this lift is compatible with the maps
F_{n}\to F_{n+1}
because of the uniqueness.

Proposition 6.16
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
and let
\mathcal{K}=\bigoplus_{K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}}K
, where we take the direct sum over all the isomorphism classes of compacts.
Then, for any
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
)
H
there exists an object
F\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4}\subseteq\mathsf{T}
and an isomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)\to H
, see
Notation

2.2
.

Proof.

By
Remark

6.15
, there exists a sequence
F_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{1}}F_{2}\xrightarrow{\beta_{2}}F_{3}\xrightarrow{\beta_{3}}F_{4}\xrightarrow{\beta_{4}}\cdots
with compatible maps
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
. Let
F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}F_{n}
. Then, the natural map
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to\mathcal{Y}(F)
is an isomorphism by
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
. So, we get a map
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)\to H
. We will now show that this map is an isomorphism.

Consider the full subcategory of
\mathsf{T}^{c}
defined as,

\mathsf{S}=\left\{E\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\Big|\ \phi(E):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E,F)\to H(E)\text{ is an isomorphism}\right\}

It is clear that
\mathsf{S}
is closed under extensions and summands in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
. We just need to show that
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{i}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
, as
\operatorname{smd}(\operatorname{coprod}(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{i}))=\mathsf{T}^{c}
by
Definition

3.8
(1). For that, consider
\phi|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}:\mathcal{Y}(F)|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
.
As
\mathcal{Y}(F)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\mathcal{Y}(F_{i})
,
\phi|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
is a map to
H|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
from the sequence,

\mathcal{Y}(F_{1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{2})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{2})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\mathcal{Y}(F_{3})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{3})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}}\cdots

By
Lemma

6.14
, the map
\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
factors as
\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to H|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{Y}(F_{n+1})|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
. So, the sequence is ind-isomorphic to the constant sequence with each term being
H|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
. And so,
\phi|_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}
is an isomorphism, and hence
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{i}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{S}
. Hence,
\phi
is an isomorphism.

It just remains to show that
F\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4}
. Note that
F=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}F_{n}
, and each
F_{n}
has a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system (see
Remark

6.15
), so they in turn are homtopy colimits of compact objects. That is,
F_{n}\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and hence

F=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}F_{n}\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{2}\star\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{2}=\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4}

and we are done.

∎

Remark 6.17
.

From now on, we will be adding the assumption that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (see
Definition

4.2
) on top of the assumptions in
Setup

6.3
. We note here that with the assumption of weak
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, the extended good metrics
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
(
Definition

3.8
) and
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
are
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent by
Proposition

4.9
for any
\mathcal{R}
in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics on
\mathsf{T}
(see
Definition

3.15
).

Lemma 6.18
.

In addition to the assumptions of
Setup

6.3
, we assume that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox, see
Definition

4.2
. Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
). Then, there exists a positive integer
\widetilde{A}
such that for each
n\geq 1
there exists

(1)

an object
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
(see
Definition

3.17
) and,

(2)

a natural transformation
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
for each
n\geq 1
such that
\phi_{n}|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
is an epimorphism.

Proof.

Firstly, as
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox and
\mathcal{R}
lies in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
), by
Proposition

4.10
, there exists an integer
A>0
such that
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
A
satisfy
Definition

4.2
(1)-(3).

Note that by
Lemma

6.14
and
Remark

6.15
, we can always find an object
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, with a map
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
, which is an epimorphism when restricted to
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Further, by
Lemma

3.20
(2),
\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
. So, the additional assertion is on the uniform bound on which
\mathcal{R}_{i}
each of the
F_{n}
is contained in.

As
H
is
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite, there exists an integer
B^{\prime}>0
such that
H(\mathcal{G}[B^{\prime},\infty))=0
, see
Notation

3.6
. And so,
H
is zero on
\mathcal{G}^{[B^{\prime},\infty)}
. By
Corollary

5.4
, there exists an integer
B>0
such that for all
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{j}
and
i>0
, there exists a triangle,
E_{i}\to F\to D_{i}\to\Sigma E_{i}
such that
E_{i}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-j-i+1,\infty)}
and
D_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{j+i}
. Let
\widetilde{A}=B+B^{\prime}+1
.

Consider the object
F_{n}
we started with. As
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
by
Lemma

3.20
(2), there exists a positive integer
l
such that
F_{n}\in\mathcal{R}_{-l}
.
By
Corollary

5.4
on
F_{n}
, with
i=l-B-B^{\prime}
, we get a triangle
E\to F_{n}\to D\to\Sigma E
with
D\in\mathcal{R}_{-l+i}=\mathcal{R}_{-(B+B^{\prime})}
and
E\in\mathcal{G}^{[B^{\prime}+1,\infty)}
. So,
H(E)=0
. And so, by the Yoneda lemma, the composite
\mathcal{Y}(E)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\alpha)}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\xrightarrow{\phi}H
vanishes.

We now apply
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
in full force, with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathsf{T}^{c}
, see also
Remark

6.13
. There exists the constant
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system for
E\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. The connecting morphisms are all identity, hence in particular are split monomorphisms, and so condition (vii) of
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
is satisfied. There exists a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system
C_{*}
for
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
by
Lemma

6.2
. And finally, we have shown in the previous paragraph that the composite
\mathcal{Y}(E)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\alpha)}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\xrightarrow{\phi}H
vanishes. Up to passing to a subsequence, the map
E\to F_{n}
induces a map between the constant sequence given by
E
and the sequence
C_{*}
. So, by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
there exists a weak triangle
E\xrightarrow{\alpha}F_{n}\xrightarrow{\beta}\widetilde{D}\to\Sigma F_{n}
, where
\widetilde{D}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}D_{i}
, where
D_{i}=\operatorname{Cone}(E\to C_{i})
(see
Remark

6.13
), such that
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
, factors as,

\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta)}\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{D})\xrightarrow{\psi}H

The surjectivity of
\phi|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
implies that of
\psi|_{\mathcal{G}_{n}}
.

Finally, we show that
\widetilde{D}
belongs to
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
. Note that
D=\operatorname{Cone}(E\to F_{n})\in\mathcal{R}_{-(B+B^{\prime})}
, and as
C_{\star}
is a strong
\mathcal{G}_{n}
-approximating system for
F
, we have that
\operatorname{Cone}(C_{i}\to F_{n})\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
for all
i
. So, by a simple application of the octahedral axiom to
E\to C_{i}\to F_{n}
we get that
D_{i}=\operatorname{Cone}(E\to C_{i})\in\mathcal{R}_{-(B+B^{\prime})}
. By
Remark

6.13
,
\widetilde{D}=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{D_{i}}\in\mathcal{R}_{-(B+B^{\prime}+1)}
. So, the requited result holds with
\widetilde{A}=B+B^{\prime}+1
.

∎

Lemma 6.19
.

In additions to the assumptions of
Setup

6.3
, we further assume that
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
Definition

4.2
). Let
B>0
be the integer we get from
Lemma

5.2
. Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
).
Assume we are give objects
\widetilde{F},F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
and
E\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and a morphism
\alpha:E\to\widetilde{F}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
, see
Definition

3.17
. Further, assume we are given a positive integer
m
, and a morphism
\widetilde{\phi}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F})\to H
, which restricts to an epimorphism on
\mathcal{G}_{mB}
and a morphism
\phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})\to H
which restricts to an epimorphism on
\mathcal{G}_{(m+1)B}
.

Then, we get the following,

(1)

There exists a commutative diagram in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
as follows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(2)

Furthermore, there exists a morphism
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime})\to H
such that :

(i)

E^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}

(ii)

\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha^{\prime}),\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma)\in\mathcal{R}_{m-1+i}

(iii)

The following diagram commutes,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

which implies that
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{G}_{(m+1)B}}
is an epimorphism.

(iv)

The following square commutes,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\gamma)}

Proof.

Firstly, as
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox and
\mathcal{R}
lies in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
), by
Proposition

4.10
, there exists an integer
A>0
such that
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
A
satisfy the
Definition

4.2
(1)-(2) and (3
′
).

We apply
Corollary

5.4
to
F^{\prime}
and
m>0
to get a triangle
E_{m}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a}F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{b}D_{m}\to\Sigma E_{m}^{\prime}
with
D_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+m}
and
E_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{[-B-m+1-i,B-i]}_{mB}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{mB}
, see
Notation

3.6
.

As
E_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, by Yoneda lemma, the map
\mathcal{Y}(E_{m}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(f)}\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\phi^{\prime}}H
corresponds to an element
x\in H(E_{m}^{\prime})
. As
E_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{mB}
, the map

\widetilde{\phi}(E_{m}^{\prime}):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(E_{m}^{\prime},\widetilde{F})=\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F})(E_{m}^{\prime})\to H(E_{m}^{\prime})

is surjective by hypothesis. So, there exists a map
f:E_{m}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{F}
such that
\widetilde{\phi}(f)=x
, which by Yoneda lemma give us the following commutative square,

(6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{m}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}

As
\widetilde{F}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
it has a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system
\widetilde{E}_{*}
by
Lemma

6.2
. Again, by
Lemma

6.2
,
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}\widetilde{E}_{i}\cong\widetilde{F}
. Consider the map
(\alpha,f):E\oplus E_{m}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{F}
. By
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
, this map factors through
\widetilde{E}_{j}
for some
j\geq 1
. We can choose
\widetilde{E}_{j}
with
j\geq m+i
, and define
E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widetilde{E}_{j}
. So, by above, we get that
(\alpha,f):E\oplus E_{m}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{F}
is given by the composite,

E\oplus E_{m}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(\varepsilon,g)}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\gamma}\widetilde{F}

By construction,
\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma)\in\mathcal{R}_{m+1+i}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
. Further, as
\widetilde{F}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, we get that
E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, as
E^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{-1}\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma)\star\widetilde{F}
. Similarly, from the triangle on the morphism
a
, along with the fact that
F^{\prime},\Sigma^{-1}\operatorname{Cone}(a)=D_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
give us that
E_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

Now, we get the following commutative diagram from
6.1
, using
f=\gamma\circ g
,

(6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{m}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho=\widetilde{\phi}\circ\mathcal{Y}(\gamma)}

From this diagram, we get a map
\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}-g\\
\ \ a\end{pmatrix}:E_{m}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
, and a morphism
(\varrho,\phi^{\prime}):\mathcal{Y}(E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime})\to H
, such that the following composite vanishes,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{m}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\varrho,\phi}^{\prime})}

E_{m}^{\prime}
is a compact object so it has a trivial strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system, given by the constant sequence.
E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, and hence by
Lemma

6.2
(3) it also has a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system. For the purposes of this proof, we make a choice of the first few terms of this sequence. We can do this as the proof of
Lemma

6.2
(3) goes via induction. It is clear that
E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}_{*}
is a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system for
E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}
for any strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system
F^{\prime}_{*}
for
F^{\prime}
. We can choose the sequence
F^{\prime}_{*}
so that
F_{n}^{\prime}=E_{m}^{\prime}
for all
n\leq m+i
with identity maps, and the map to
F^{\prime}
being
a:E_{m}^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}
, as
\operatorname{Cone}(a)=D_{m}\in\mathcal{R}_{i+m}
. This is our choice of the approximating system for
E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}
.

By
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 6.5]
and
Remark

6.13
, we can complete the map
\sigma
to a weak triangle in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{m}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma E_{m}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

such that
(\varrho,\phi^{\prime})=\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{Y}((\alpha^{\prime},\beta))
for a morphism
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime})\to H
.

Recall that the strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system
\widetilde{F}_{*}^{\prime}
we get for
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
by
Remark

6.13
is given by
\widetilde{F}_{n}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Cone}(E_{m}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}\oplus F_{n+1}^{\prime})
. In particular, for
n=m+i-1
, as
F^{\prime}_{m+i}=E_{m}^{\prime}
by the previous paragraph, we get that,

\widetilde{F}_{m+i-1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Cone}(E_{m}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}\oplus F_{m+i}^{\prime}=E^{\prime}\oplus E_{m}^{\prime})\cong E^{\prime}

and as can be easily checked, the map from
E^{\prime}\oplus F_{m+i}^{\prime}
to
\widetilde{F}_{m+i-1}^{\prime}
is exactly the projection map. This gives us that the obvious map
\widetilde{F}_{m+i-1}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
is the same as
\alpha^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
, which implies that
\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{m+i-1}
. Further, as we already know that
E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}
, this gives us that
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
is in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

From
6.2
and the fact that
(\varrho,\phi^{\prime})
factors through
\mathcal{Y}((\alpha^{\prime},\beta))
, Yoneda Lemma gives us the following commutative square in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{m}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

Further, we also get the following commutative diagram from above,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{m}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

which shows (iii) and (iv) completing the proof.

∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Suppose we are in
Setup

6.3
. Further assume that
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
Definition

4.2
). Let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
). Then, there exists an object
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) and an epimorphism
\mathcal{Y}(F)\to H
.

Proof.

As
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable and
\mathcal{R}
lies in the preferred equivalence class of orthogonal metrics (
Definition

3.15
), by
Proposition

4.10
, there exists an integer
A>0
such that
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
A
satisfy the
Definition

4.2
(1)-(2) and (3
′
).

By
Lemma

6.18
, there exists a positive integer
\widetilde{A}
, and for each positive integer
n
, an object
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
and a morphism
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\to H
which is an epimorphism on
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Let
B>0
be the integer we get from
Lemma

5.2
.

We will use induction to produce in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
a sequence,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{4}}

such that for all
i\geq 1
,
E_{i}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and there exist maps
\widetilde{\phi}_{i}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i})\to H
satisfying,

(1)

\widetilde{\phi}_{i}|_{\mathcal{G}_{(i+3)B}}
is an epimorphism.

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{i+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\phi}}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}_{i+1}}

commutes for each
i\geq 1
.

(3)

\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma_{i}),\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha_{i})\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1-\widetilde{A}}

We proceed via induction on
n\geq 1
. For
n=1
, we define
\widetilde{F}_{1}=F_{4B}
, and
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}=\phi_{4B}
. As
\widetilde{F}_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, there exists a triangle
E_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}\widetilde{F}_{1}\to D_{1}\to\Sigma E_{1}
with
E_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{1-\widetilde{A}}
. As
\widetilde{F}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
, we get that
E_{1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
. This proves the result for
n=1

Now assume we know the result for up to a positive integer
n
. In particular, we have a map
\alpha_{n}:E_{n}\to\widetilde{F}_{n}
in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
, with
E_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha_{n})\in\mathcal{R}_{n+1-\widetilde{A}}
, and a map
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{n})\to H
which is an epimorphism on
\mathcal{G}_{(n+3)B}
.

Again, by
Lemma

6.18
, there exists an object
F_{(n+4)B}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
with a morphism
\phi_{(n+4)B}:\mathcal{Y}(F_{(n+4)B})\to H
, such that the restriction to
\mathcal{G}_{(n+4)B}
is a surjection. By applying
Lemma

6.19
with
m=n+3
and
i=-\widetilde{A}
, we get in
\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{-\widetilde{A}}
a diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{(n+4)B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{n+1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

as well as a map
\widetilde{\phi}_{n+1}:\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{n+1})\to H
satisfying the properties listed in the statement of
Lemma

6.19
.
Lemma

6.19
(i) gives us that
E_{n+1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.
Lemma

6.19
(ii) tells us that
\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma_{n+1}),\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha_{n+1})\in\mathcal{R}_{m-1+i}=\mathcal{R}_{n+2-\widetilde{A}}=\mathcal{R}_{(n+1)+1-\widetilde{A}}
.
Lemma

6.19
(iii) says that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n+1}|_{\mathcal{G}_{(n+4)B}}
is an epimorphism. And finally,
Lemma

6.19
(iv) tells us that the square in (2) commutes. This completes the induction process.

So, we have a sequence
E_{1}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{1}}E_{2}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{2}}E_{3}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{3}}\cdots
. We define the map
\psi_{i}:\mathcal{Y}(E_{i})\to H
to be the composite
\mathcal{Y}(E_{i})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{i})}\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\phi}_{i}}H
. Next, we prove that the following diagram commutes,

(6.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(E_{i+1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}(E_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\varepsilon_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}

Notice that the commutativity of
6.3
is the same as the commutativity of the boundary of the diagram
6.4
below,

(6.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{i+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(E_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\widetilde{F}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\varepsilon_{i+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}(\alpha_{i})}

The square in the above diagram commutes by (2), and the triangle commutes as the diagram
6.5
below commutes,

(6.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}

This gives us the commutativity of the boundary of
6.4
, and hence the commutativity of
6.3
. This in turn helps us define a map to
H
as follows. Let
F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}
. We define
\psi:\mathcal{Y}(F)\to H
as the colimit of
\psi_{i}
, noting that
\mathcal{Y}(F)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\mathcal{Y}(E_{i})
by
[
Nee96
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Now applying the octahedral axiom to
6.5
, we get that
\operatorname{Cone}(\varepsilon_{i+1})\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1-\widetilde{A}}
, as
\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha_{i}),\Sigma^{-1}\operatorname{Cone}(\gamma_{i+1})\in\mathcal{R}_{i+1-\widetilde{A}}
, which gives us that up to passing to a subsequence,
E_{*}
is a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system (
Definition

6.1
), and so, by
Lemma

6.2
,
F=\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{i}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Finally, it remains to show that
\psi
is an epimorphism. Let
C\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. As
\mathcal{G}
is a generating sequence (see
Definition

3.8
(1)), we have that
\mathsf{T}=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\mathcal{G}_{i}
, see
Notation

3.6
. So,
C\in\mathcal{G}_{(n+3)B}
for some
n>0
. By
Lemma

3.21
, we can further choose
n
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,\mathcal{R}_{n+1-\widetilde{A}})=0
. So, we get that the map
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,\widetilde{F}_{n})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(C)}H(C)
is surjective as
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}|_{\mathcal{G}_{(n+3)B}}
is an epimorphism. We know that
\widetilde{D}_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Cone}(\alpha_{n})\in\mathcal{R}_{n+1-\widetilde{A}}
. So, we get the exact sequence,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,E_{n})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,\alpha_{n})}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,\widetilde{F}_{n})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,\widetilde{D}_{n})=0

So,
\psi_{n}(C):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,E_{n})\to H(C)
is surjective as it is a composite of two surjective morphism, namely
\operatorname{Hom}(C,\alpha_{n})
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}(C)
. But,
\psi_{n}(C)
factors via
\psi(C):\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,F)\to H(C)
, that is
\psi_{n}(C)=\operatorname{Hom}(C,F_{n})\to\operatorname{Hom}(C,F)\xrightarrow{\psi(C)}H(C)
, so
\psi(C)
also has to be surjective. As this is true for any
C\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we are done.

∎

Corollary 6.21
.

In addition to the conditions of
Setup

6.3
, let
\mathsf{T}
be
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox (
Definition

4.2
). Let
F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}
be an object such that
H=\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
). Then, there exists a triangle
F\xrightarrow{f}F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}D\to\Sigma F
with
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) and
g
a phantom map, see
Definition

2.9
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.20
, we get
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and an epimorphism
\phi:\mathcal{Y}(F)\to H=\mathcal{Y}(F^{\prime})
. By
Lemma

6.2
(3), there exists a strong approximating system for
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. So, by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 5.8]
, there exists a map
f:F\to F^{\prime}
so that
\phi=\mathcal{Y}(f)
. Consider the triangle
F\xrightarrow{f}F^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}D\to\Sigma F
we get from completing
f
to a triangle. For any object
C\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we get an exact sequence,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,F)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,f)}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,F^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,g)}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(C,D)

As the first map is surjective, we get that the second map is zero, and hence
g
is a phantom.

∎

Now we state and prove the the main results of this chapter.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable triangulated category (
Definition

4.2
) for a finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
). Further, assume that
\mathsf{T}
is
R
-linear for a commutative noetherian ring
R
, and that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,G^{\prime})
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
G,G^{\prime}
in
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. Then, the functor
\mathcal{Y}:\mathsf{T}^{c}\to

\operatorname{Hom}\big([\mathsf{T}^{c}]^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Mod}(R)\big)
taking
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) to
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(-,F)|_{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, satisfies the following :

(1)

The essential image of
\mathcal{Y}
is exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functors, see
Definition

6.6
.

(2)

\mathcal{Y}
is full.

Proof.

We already know that
\mathcal{Y}(F)
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor for any
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
by
Lemma

6.14
. By
Lemma

6.2
(3), any
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
admits a strong
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-approximating system (
Definition

6.1
), and hence by
[
Nee21b
, Lemma 5.8]
, the functor
\mathcal{Y}
is full on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Now, let
H
be any
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor. By
Proposition

6.16
, there exists
F\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4}
such that
H\cong\mathcal{Y}(F)
. Recall that
\mathcal{K}=\bigoplus_{K\in\mathsf{T}^{c}}K
, where the coproduct is over the equivalence class of compact objects, and hence a set-indexed coproduct. It remains to show that
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
.

Denote the ideal of phantom maps by
\mathcal{I}
(
Definition

2.9
). We use induction to prove that for any
n\geq 1
, there exists a triangle
F_{n}\to F\xrightarrow{\beta_{n}}D_{n}\to\Sigma F_{n}
with
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
\beta_{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{n}
.
n=1
is given by
Corollary

6.21
. So, assume now that we know the result up to a positive integer
n
, i.e. a triangle
F_{n}\to F\xrightarrow{\beta_{n}}D_{n}\to\Sigma F_{n}
with
F_{n}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
\beta_{n}\in\mathcal{I}^{n}
. Consider the exact sequence,

\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma^{-1}F)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma^{-1}\beta_{n})\ =\ 0\ }\mathcal{Y}(\Sigma^{-1}D_{n})\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{Y}(F)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\beta_{n})\ =\ 0\ }\mathcal{Y}(D_{n})

As
\mathcal{Y}(F_{n})
and
\mathcal{Y}(F)
are both
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functors, the above exact sequence tells us that so is
\mathcal{Y}(D_{n})
. So, by
Corollary

6.21
, we have a triangle
F^{\prime}\to D_{n}\xrightarrow{\gamma}D_{n+1}\to\Sigma F^{\prime}
with
F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{I}
. Let
\beta_{n+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\gamma\circ\beta_{n}
. Note that
\beta_{n+1}\in\mathcal{I}^{n+1}
. Consider the triangle
F_{n+1}\to F\xrightarrow{\beta_{n+1}}D_{n+1}\to\Sigma F_{n+1}
we get from
\beta_{n+1}
. Applying the octahedral axiom to
\gamma\circ\beta_{n}
, we get a triangle
F_{n}\to F_{n+1}\to F^{\prime}\to\Sigma F_{n}
. By
Proposition

3.22
we get that
F_{n+1}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
as
F_{n},F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, which completes the induction argument.

Now, consider the triangle
F_{4}\to F\to D_{4}\to\Sigma F_{4}
. Note that
F\to D_{4}\in\mathcal{I}^{4}
and
F\in\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4}
.
(\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{1},\mathcal{I})
forms a projective class, see
Definition

2.8
. And so, by
[
Chr98
, Theorem 1.1]
, so does
(\overline{\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle}_{4},\mathcal{I}^{4})
. This gives us that
F\to D_{4}
vanishes, and hence
F
is a direct summand of
F_{4}\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
. As
\mathsf{T}^{c}
is thick by
Proposition

3.23
, we get
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, and we are done.

∎

Lemma 6.23
.

With the same assumptions as
Theorem

6.22
, let
f:F\to F^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{T}^{c}
, with
F^{\prime}\in T^{b}_{c}
, see
Definition

3.17
. Then
\mathcal{Y}(f)=0
implies
f=0
.

Proof.

As
F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}
, there exists an integer
n
such that
F^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n})^{\perp}=(\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n})^{\perp}
, see
Definition

3.8
. As
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we have a triangle
E\xrightarrow{g}F\xrightarrow{h}D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
. As
\mathcal{Y}(f)=0
, we get that
fg=0
, which tells us that
f:F\to F^{\prime}
factors as
F\xrightarrow{h}D\to F^{\prime}
. As
D\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
and
F^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n})^{\perp}
,
(D\to F^{\prime})=0
, and hence so is
f
, and we are done.

∎

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable triangulated category (
Definition

4.2
) for a finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
). Further, assume that
\mathsf{T}
is
R
-linear for a commutative noetherian ring
R
, and that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(G,G^{\prime})
is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
G,G^{\prime}
in
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{n}
. Then, the functor
\mathcal{Y}|_{\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}}:\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}\to

\operatorname{Hom}\big([\mathsf{T}^{c}]^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Mod}(R)\big)
taking
F\in\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}
(
Definition

3.17
) to
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(-,F)|_{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
, satisfies the following :

(1)

The essential image of
\mathcal{Y}|_{\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}}
is exactly the
\mathcal{G}
-finite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functors, see
Definition

6.6
.

(2)

\mathcal{Y}|_{\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

It just remains to identify the essential image of
\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}
under
\mathcal{Y}
, as
Theorem

6.22
and
Lemma

6.23
show that
\mathcal{Y}|_{\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}}
is fully faithful.

Let
F\in\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}\subseteq\mathsf{T}^{c}
, see
Definition

3.17
. By
Lemma

6.9
, we already know that
\mathcal{Y}(F)
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor (
Definition

6.6
). By
Definition

3.17
,
\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}=\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\big(\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}\big)^{\perp}
see
Remark

6.17
. And so,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i},F)=0
for
i>>0
. Therefore,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{-i},F)=0
for
i>>0
as
\mathcal{G}^{i}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, see
Definition

3.17
. This gives us that
\mathcal{Y}(F)
is a bounded
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor for any
F\in\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}
.

Conversely, let
H
be a
\mathcal{G}
-finite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor. As this implies that
H
is a
\mathcal{G}
-semifinite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor, by
Theorem

6.22
(1) there exists
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
such that
H\cong\mathcal{Y}(F)
. As
H
is a
\mathcal{G}
-finite
\mathsf{T}^{c}
-cohomological functor, there exists a positive integer
n
such that
H(\mathcal{G}^{-i})=0
for all
i\geq n
. And so, as
H\cong\mathcal{Y}(F)
, we get that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{-i},F)=0
for all
i\geq n
. So,
F\in(\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n})^{\perp}
, see
Definition

3.17
. As
F\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
already, we have shown that
F\in\mathsf{T}^{b}_{c}=\mathsf{T}^{c}\cap\big(\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}\big)^{\perp}
, and we are done.
∎

7.
Examples of approximability

In this section we discuss some examples of (weakly)
\mathcal{G}
-approximable and (weakly)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapproximable triangulated categories, see
Definition

4.1
and
Definition

4.2
. We first focus on an abstract class of examples, and later, we give more concrete examples.

We have already seen in
Theorem

4.14
that approximable triangulated categories are examples of
\mathcal{G}
-approximable triangulated categories. We begin by introducing another class of examples of
\mathcal{G}
-approximable triangulated categories, namely co-approximable triangulated categories. In this setting, co-t-structures will play the role t-structures do for approximability.

We begin with some definitions.

Definition 7.1
.

Two co-t-structures
(\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{V}_{1})
and
(\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{V}_{2})
on a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
are said to be equivalent if there exists a positive integer
A
such that,

\Sigma^{-A}\mathsf{U}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{U}_{2}\subseteq\Sigma^{A}\mathsf{U}_{1}

Equivalently, by taking orthogonals and shifting,

\Sigma^{A}\mathsf{V}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{V}_{2}\subseteq\Sigma^{-A}\mathsf{V}_{1}

It is easy to verify that this indeed gives an equivalence relation on the collection of co-t-structures on
\mathsf{T}
.

Recall that we can construct a co-t-structure on a triangulated category with coproducts from a given set of compact objects, see
Theorem

2.7
. We will in particular be interested in co-t-structures generated by a single compact object.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with all small coproducts. Then for any
H\in\mathsf{T}^{c}
, we define
\mathsf{V}_{H}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{-n}H:n\geq 1\}^{\perp}
. Then, the
co-t-structure generated by
H
is defined to be
(\mathsf{U}_{H},\mathsf{V}_{H})
, where
\mathsf{U}_{H}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ ^{\perp}(\Sigma\mathsf{V}_{H})
. It is a co-t-structure by
Theorem

2.7
.

Remark 7.3
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We observe that we are in the setting of
Section

3
by considering the finite generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
)
\mathcal{G}
given by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
. The metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
, see
Definition

3.8
, is then given by
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n}=\Sigma^{-n}\mathsf{U}_{G}
where
\mathsf{U}_{G}
is as defined in
Definition

7.2
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathsf{T}
be two compact generators of a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
. Then, the co-t-structures
(\mathsf{U}_{G_{1}},\mathsf{V}_{G_{1}})
and
(\mathsf{U}_{G_{2}},\mathsf{V}_{G_{2}})
these generate are equivalent.

Proof.

Follows from
Remark

7.3
,
Lemma

3.14
(2), and
Example

3.13
.
∎

This motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. Then, we define the
preferred equivalence class of co-t-structures on
\mathsf{T}
to be all co-t-structures which are equivalent to
(\mathsf{U}_{G},\mathsf{V}_{G})
, see
Definition

7.2
. Note that by
Lemma

7.4
, this definition is independent of the choice of the compact generator
G\in\mathsf{T}
.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
be a co-t-structure on
\mathsf{T}
. We define the following full subcategories,

\mathsf{T}^{-}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{n}\mathsf{V}\ ,\ \mathsf{T}_{c}^{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathsf{T}^{c}\star\Sigma^{-n}\mathsf{U})

It is clear that these categories remain the same if replace the co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
by an equivalent one. Hence, if
\mathsf{T}
has a single compact generator,
\mathsf{T}^{-}
and
\mathsf{T}_{c}^{+}
defined by a co-t-structure in the preferred equivalence class (
Definition

7.5
) are intrinsic subcategories of
\mathsf{T}
.

The following remark is just an exercise in unwinding definitions.

Remark 7.7
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We define the finite generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
)
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, there is a bijection between the preferred equivalence class of co-t-structures on
\mathsf{T}
and those metrics
\mathcal{M}
in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metrics, such that
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathsf{U}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
for a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
on
\mathsf{T}
.

Now, we come to the definition of co-approximability.

Definition 7.8
.

We say a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
is
weakly co-approximable
if there exists a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
, a compact generator
G
, and an integer
A>0
such that,

(1)

\Sigma^{-A}G\in\mathsf{U}
,

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{A}G,\mathsf{U})=0
, and

(3)

Every object
F\in\mathsf{U}
admits a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
such that
D\in\Sigma^{-1}\mathsf{U}

\text{ and }E\in\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A,A]}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

We say
\mathsf{T}
is
co-approximable
if further,

(3)
′

Every object
E\in\mathsf{U}
admits a triangle

E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
such that
D\in\Sigma^{-1}\mathsf{U}
and
E\in\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A,A]}_{A}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

Definition 7.9
.

We say a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
is
weakly co-quasiapproximable
if there exists a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
, a compact generator
G
, and an integer
A>0
such that,

(1)

\Sigma^{-A}G\in\mathsf{U}
,

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{A}G,\mathsf{U})=0
, and

(3)

Every object
F\in\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}
admits a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
such that
D\in\Sigma^{-1}\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}

\text{ and }E\in\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A,A]}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

We say
\mathsf{T}
is
co-quasiapproximable
if further,

(3)
′

Every object
E\in\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}
admits a triangle

E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
such that
D\in\Sigma^{-1}\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}
and
E\in\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A,A]}_{A}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

It is easy to verify that if
\mathsf{T}
is (weakly) co-approx (resp. (weakly) co-quasiapprox) with respect to one compact generator, then it is (weakly) co-approx (resp. (weakly) co-quasiapprox) with respect to any other compact generator up to increasing the integer
A
if necessary.

The following result shows that co-approximability is a special case of
\mathcal{G}
-approximability.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We define the finite generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
)
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
. Then,

(i)

\mathsf{T}
is a weakly co-approximable triangulated category (
Definition

7.8
) if and only if
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
).

(ii)

\mathsf{T}
is a co-approximable triangulated category (
Definition

7.8
) if and only if
\mathsf{T}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
).

The analogous result also holds for (weak) co-quasiapprox and (weak)
\mathcal{G}
-quasiapprox.

Proof.

Both (i) and (ii) follow the same way, so we only prove (i). Suppose
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable. Then, by
Remark

7.3
we get that that the co-t-structure compactly generated by
G
gives
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
under the bijection given in
Remark

7.7
, which is a metric in the preferred
\mathbb{N}
-equivalence class of orthogonal metric. By
Proposition

4.10
,
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable with respect to this orthogonal metric. It is easy to see that this gives us that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly co-approximable using the co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U}_{G},\mathsf{V}_{G})
, see
Definition

7.2
.
Definition

4.1
(1), (2), and (3) imply
Definition

7.8
(1), (2), and (3) respectively.

Conversely, suppose
\mathsf{T}
is weakly co-approximable. So, there exists a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
, and an integer
A>0
such that the conditions (1)-(3) of
Definition

7.8
are satisfied with the compact generator
G
. We will show that
\mathsf{T}
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable by showing the conditions (0)-(3) of
Remark

4.3
hold with the orthogonal good metric
\mathcal{R}_{i}=\Sigma^{-i}\mathsf{U}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and the integer
A>0
.

(0)

As
\mathcal{G}^{n}=\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
, we get that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\mathcal{G}^{(-\infty,-i]}=\langle G\rangle^{[i,\infty)}
by definition. See
Definition

3.8
(2) for the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Also see,
Notation

3.6
and
Notation

2.2
.

(1)

As
\Sigma^{-A}G\in\mathsf{U}=\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
\mathcal{M}^{G}_{i+A}=\langle G\rangle^{[i+A,\infty)}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

(2)

As
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{n}G,\mathsf{U})=0
for all
n\geq A
, we get that,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{n}G,\Sigma^{-i}\mathsf{U})=0

for all
n+i\geq A
.

(3)

For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}=\Sigma^{-i}\mathsf{U}
, we get that
\Sigma^{i}F\in\mathsf{U}
. So, by the
Definition

7.8
(3), we get a triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
with
E\in\langle G\rangle^{[-A+i,A+i]}
and
D\in\Sigma^{-i-1}\mathsf{U}=\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
, which is what we needed.

∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a weakly co-approximable triangulated category, with a compact generator
G
, co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
and positive integer
A
satisfying the conditions (1)-(3) of
Definition

7.8
. Then,
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
is in the preferred equivalence class of co-t-structures on
\mathsf{T}
. Conversely, for any co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\mathsf{V}^{\prime})
in the preferred equivalence class of co-t-structures (
Definition

7.5
), there exists an integer
A^{\prime}
such that the conditions of weak co-approximability are satisfied.

Proof.

Follows from
Theorem

7.10
,
Proposition

4.9
and
Remark

7.7
. The converse follows from
Proposition

4.10
.
∎

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a weakly co-approximable triangulated category, with compact generator
G
, co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
and positive integer
A
satisfying the conditions (1)-(3) of
Definition

7.8
. Then,
\mathsf{U}\subseteq\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A-1,\infty)}
, see
Notation

2.2
. In particular,
\mathsf{U}_{G}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{}^{\perp}(G[0,\infty)^{\perp})\subseteq\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A-1,\infty)}
, see
Definition

7.2
and
Proposition

7.11
.

Proof.

Follows from
Theorem

7.10
and
Corollary

4.8
.
∎

We now see what the closure of the compacts and the orthogonal to
\mathcal{G}
are in this setting, see
Definition

3.17
.

The following is again just an exercise in unwinding definitions.

Remark 7.13
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with a single compact generator
G
. We define the finite generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{n}G\}
. Then,
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}=\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\perp}=\mathsf{T}^{-}
, see
Definition

7.6
,
Remark

7.3
, and
Definition

3.17
.

We note the following results about the closure of the compacts in this setting.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
a compactly generated triangulated category with a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
. Assume there exists a compact generator
G
and a positive integer
A
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{A}G,\mathsf{U})=0
. Then
\mathsf{T}_{c}^{+}
(
Definition

7.6
) is triangulated.

Proof.

Follows from
Remark

7.3
and
Proposition

3.22

∎

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
a compactly generated triangulated category with a co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
. Assume there exists a compact generator
G
and a positive integer
A
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma^{A}G,\mathsf{U})=0
and
\Sigma^{-A}G\subseteq\mathsf{U}
. Then
\mathsf{T}_{c}^{+}
(
Definition

7.6
) is thick.

Proof.

Follows from
Remark

7.3
and
Proposition

3.23
.
∎

Corollary 7.16
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a weakly co-approximable triangulated category, with compact generator
G
, co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
and positive integer
A
satisfying
Definition

7.8
(1)-(3). Then, for any object
F\in\mathsf{T}^{+}_{c}\cap\mathsf{U}
(
Definition

7.6
), there exists a sequence
\{E_{i}\to E_{i+1}\}_{i\geq 1}
mapping to
F
such that
E_{i}\in\langle G\rangle^{[-B,B+i-1]}
and
\operatorname{Cone}(E_{i}\to F)\in\Sigma^{-i}\mathsf{U}
for all
i\geq 1
. If
\mathsf{T}
is co-approximable, we can further arrange the sequence
E_{*}
so that
E_{i}\in\langle G\rangle^{[-B,B+i-1]}_{iB}
, see
Notation

2.2
.

The analogous result also holds for (weak) quasiapprox. In all of these cases, the non-canonical map
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}{E}_{i}\to F
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Follows from
Remark

7.3
,
Theorem

7.10
, and
Corollary

5.4
.
∎

7.1.
Examples coming from algebraic geometry

Now, we give a class of examples of co-approximable triangulated categories, see
Definition

7.8
. We begin with some conventions.

Notation 7.17
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. Then,

(1)

(\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))^{\leq 0},\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))^{\geq 0})
denotes the standard t-structure on
\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))
, where the truncation triangles are given by the soft or the canonical truncation of complexes.

(2)

(\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\geq 0},\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\leq 0})
denotes the standard co-t-structure on
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)
, where the truncation triangles are given by the hard or the brutal truncation of complexes.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme and
p:\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)\to\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))
be the Verdier quotient map, see
[
Kra05
, Proposition 3.6]
. Then, this restricts to an equivalence
p:\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\geq 0}\to\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))^{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

This follows trivially as
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)}(E,F)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}({\operatorname{Qcoh}}(X))}(E,F)
if
F
is a bounded below complex of injective quasicoherent sheaves, and
E
is an arbitrary complex of injective quasicoherent sheaves, see
[
Sta24
, Tag 05TG]
.
∎

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. Assume there exists
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
, see
Notation

2.2
. Then
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)
is co-approximable, see
Definition

7.8
.

Proof.

We work with the standard co-t-structure on
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)
again. The only non-trivial axiom to verify is the existence of approximating triangles (
Definition

7.8
(3
′
)). So, let
F\in\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\geq 0}\cong\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq 0}
, see
Lemma

7.18
. We now work in the category
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))
. Consider the truncation triangle
F^{\leq 0}\to F\to F^{\geq 1}\to\Sigma F^{\leq 0}
with respect to the standard t-structure on
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))
. We have that
F^{\leq 0}\in\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\leq 0}\cap\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq 0}
. So, by
[
Nee18b
, Lemma 2.4]
, there exists a positive integer
A
, independent of the complex
F\in\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq 0}
, such that
F^{\leq 0}\in\overline{\langle G\rangle}^{[-A,A]}_{A}
. As
F^{\geq 1}\in\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq 1}\cong\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\geq 1}
, the truncation triangle is the one we were after to prove co-approximability. Note that
G
is a classical generator for
\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
by
[
Nee21a
, Lemma 2.7]
, and hence is a compact generator for
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)
.
∎

Remark 7.20
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. Assume there exists
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
. By the proof of
Theorem

7.19
,
Proposition

4.9
, and
Theorem

7.10
, we get that the standard co-t-structures on
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)
lies in the preferred equivalence class of co-t-structures, see
Definition

7.5
. This in turn implies that the metric given by
\{\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\geq n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is equivalent to the metric given by
\{\langle G\rangle^{[n,\infty)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. This also follows immediately from
[
Nee18b
, Lemma 2.3]

Remark 7.21
.

We note here that the arguments in
Theorem

7.19
and
Remark

7.20
hold in the more general setting of the homotopy category of injective objects of a locally noetherian Grothendieck abelian category using
[
Kra05
]
. We give two important cases where this is applicable.

(1)

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a coherent algebra over
X
. Assume there exists
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(\mathcal{A}))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
, see
Notation

2.2
. Then
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}\mathcal{A})
is co-approximable and the standard co-t-structure lies in the preferred equivalence class.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a noetherian stack. Again, if there exists an object
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(\mathcal{X})
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(\mathcal{X}))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
, then
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}\mathcal{X})
is co-approximable and the standard co-t-structure lies in the preferred equivalence class.

We now give a class of example of weak co-quasiapproximable triangulated categories. The example in question will be of the mock homotopy category of projectives. Throughout our discussion, we will try to stick to the notation in
[
Mur08
]
.

We begin by setting up some conventions.

Convention 7.22
.

Let
X
be a noetherian separated scheme. We define a compactly generated co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
on the mock homotopy category of projectives
\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)
defined by the set of compact objects
U_{\lambda}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\circ}
, where
U_{\lambda}(-)^{\circ}:\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}(X))^{c}
, see
[
Mur08
, Theorem 7.4]
. This immediately gives us an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
given by
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\Sigma^{-n}\mathsf{U}
. By
[
Bon22
, Theorem 2.3.4]
, the metric
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
is
\mathbb{N}
-equivalent to the metric
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
given by
\mathcal{M}_{n}=U_{\lambda}(D^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X)^{\leq-n})
.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
X
be a noetherian, separated scheme. Then, the closure of the compacts
\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)^{+}_{c}=U_{\lambda}(\mathbf{D}^{-}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\circ}
, where we take the closure of the compacts with respect to the metric
\mathcal{R}
of
Convention

7.22
.

Proof.

One inclusion is easier to show. For any
F\in D^{-}(\operatorname{coh}(X))
and any positive integer
n
, we have a triangle
F^{\leq-{n+1}}\to F\to F^{\geq n}\to\Sigma F^{\leq-{n+1}}
given by the canonical truncation. Applying
U_{\lambda}(-)^{\circ}
to these triangles gives us that
U_{\lambda}(F)^{\circ}\in\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)^{+}_{c}
.

For the other inclusion, first note that if we define the generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{G}_{n}=U_{\lambda}(\Sigma^{n}\operatorname{coh}(X))
, then we are in the setting of
Lemma

6.2
. Let
F\in\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)^{+}_{c}
. Then, there exists a strong
\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)^{c}
-approximating sequence
\{F_{n}\to F_{n+1}\}
such that
\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}F_{n}\cong F
. By the description of compact objects, we have that for all
n
, there exists
E_{n}\in\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))
with
F_{n}=U_{\lambda}(E_{n})^{\circ}
. This gives us a triangle,

\bigoplus U_{\lambda}(E_{n})^{\circ}\to\bigoplus U_{\lambda}(E_{n})^{\circ}\to F\to\bigoplus\Sigma U_{\lambda}(E_{n})^{\circ}

By working locally, and comparing the triangles we get by applying
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}\textit{om}}(-,\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
U(-^{\circ})
to the above triangle gives us that
F
is locally a complex of vector bundles (that is, affine locally it is isomorphic to a complex of finitely generated projective modules in
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}R)
). From this, it is easy to show that
U(F^{\circ})=E
for some
E\in\mathbf{D}^{-}(\operatorname{coh}(X))
, which is what we needed to show.
∎

Theorem 7.24
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. Assume there exists
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
, see
Notation

2.2
. Then
\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)
is co-quasiapprox, see
Definition

7.9
.

Proof.

We first note that
G
is a classical generator for
\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
by
[
Nee21a
, Lemma 2.7]
, and hence,
U_{\lambda}(G)^{\circ}
is a compact generator for
\mathbf{K}_{m}(\operatorname{Proj}\text{-}X)
. The first two conditions of
Definition

7.9
are easy enough to verify with the co-t-structure
(\mathsf{U},\mathsf{V})
as in
Convention

7.22
. So, it remains to verify the final condition.

Let
F\in\mathsf{U}
. Then, by
Lemma

7.23
,
F=U_{\lambda}(\widetilde{F})^{\circ}
for some
\widetilde{F}\in\mathbf{D}^{-}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\leq 1}
. We have a triangle
\widetilde{F}^{\leq 2}\to\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{F}^{\geq 1}\to\Sigma\widetilde{F}^{\leq 2}
via the canonical truncation. Note that,

\widetilde{F}^{\geq-1}\in\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\geq-1}\cap\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\leq 1}\subseteq\langle G\rangle_{A}^{[-A,A]}

where the inclusion holds by
[
Nee18b
, Lemma 2.4]
. Applying
U(-)^{\circ}
we get the required triangle.

∎

Remark 7.25
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. Assume there exists
G\in\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
for some positive integer
N
. By the proof of
Theorem

7.19
,
Proposition

4.9
, and
Theorem

7.10
, we get that the metric given by
\{\mathbf{D}^{b}(\operatorname{coh}(X))^{\leq-n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is equivalent to the metric given by
\{\langle G\rangle^{(-\infty,-n]}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. This also follows immediately from
[
Nee18b
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Theorem 7.26
.

Let
X
be a separated noetherian scheme. Then there exists a complex
G
in
\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(X)
and a positive integer
N
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
in the following cases,

(1)

X
is regular and finite dimensional.

(2)

X
is a quasiexcellent and finite dimensional scheme.

Further, in either of these case, for any Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a complex
G
in
\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(\mathcal{A})
and a positive integer
N
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(\mathcal{A}))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
by
[
DDLMR24b
, Corollary 6.1 & Corollary 6.2]
.

Note that the homotopy category of injectives is co-approximable in these cases by
Theorem

7.19
and
Remark

7.21
.

Proof.

(1)

If
X
is regular and finite dimensional, this is well known, see for example
[
Rou08
, Theorem 7.5]
or
[
Nee21c
, Theorem 7.5]
.

(2)

This is shown in
[
Aok21
, Proof of Main Theorem, page 137]
.

∎

The following is the analogous result for stacks.

Theorem 7.27
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a noetherian, separated, finite-dimensional, quasiexcellent tame algebraic stack. Then, there exists a complex
G
in
\mathbf{D^{b}_{\operatorname{coh}}}(\mathcal{X})
and a positive integer
N
such that
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(\mathcal{X}))=\overline{\langle G\rangle}_{N}
.

Note that the homotopy category of injectives is co-approximable by
Theorem

7.19
and
Remark

7.21
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Aok21
, Corollary 4.5 & Theorem 5.1]
and
[
HP24
, Lemma B.2]
by the same proof idea as
[
HP24
, Theorem B.1]

∎

7.2.
Miscellaneous examples

We now give two examples where the generating sequence does not arise from a single compact generator. Hence, in particular these are not examples of approximability or co-approximability.

The first example is of the derived category of the classifying stack of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
over the field
\mathbb{C}
. The derived category
\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
is exceptionally simple here. We start with the structure of quasicoherent sheaves on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which is well known in the literature, see for example
[
Sch18
, Theorem 2.1 and Theorem 2.15]
.

Lemma 7.28
.

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
G_{i}
be the quasicoherent sheaf corresponding to the 1-dimensional weight
i
representation. Then,

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}(G_{i},G_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}(G_{i},G_{i})\cong\mathbb{C}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

Any quasicoherent sheaf
F
is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of
G_{i}
, that is,
F\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{i}^{\oplus I_{i}}
for sets
I_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

This gives us that in the derived category,
\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
, we have that,

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})}(\Sigma^{n}G_{i},G_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})}(\Sigma^{n}G_{i},G_{i})=0
for all
n\neq 0
and for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})}(G_{i},G_{i})\cong\mathbb{C}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 7.29
.

By
Lemma

7.28
,
\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})=\operatorname{Add}(\{\Sigma^{j}G_{i}\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}})
. We have a finite generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
)
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
, given by

\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{n+|i|}G_{i}:|i|\leq-n\}\cup\{\Sigma^{-n-|i|}G_{i}:|i|\leq-n\}

Note that
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\varnothing
for all positive integers
n
.

We can explicitly compute the extended good metric
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
(see
Definition

3.8
(2)) and
\overline{\langle G\rangle}{[-n,n]}
(see
Notation

3.6
) for this generating sequence,

(1)

\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-n]=\{\Sigma^{j}G_{i}:|j|+|i|\geq|n|\}
for all
n\leq 0
, and
\mathcal{G}(-\infty,n]=\mathcal{G}(-\infty,0]
for all
n\geq 0
.

(2)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n}=\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-n]),\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{n}=\operatorname{Add}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-n])
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

For any
n\geq 0
,
\mathcal{G}[-n,n]=\{\Sigma^{j}G_{i}:|j|+|i|\leq n\}
.

(4)

For all
n\geq 0
,
\overline{\langle G\rangle}{[-n,n]}=\operatorname{Add}(\mathcal{G}[-n,n])
.

Theorem 7.30
.

The derived category of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
), with
\mathcal{G}
being defined as in
Remark

7.29
.

Proof.

We will show
\mathcal{G}
-approximability using the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
(see
Definition

3.8
for the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}
) and the integer
A=1
. We verify below the conditions of
Remark

4.3
,

(0)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\bigcup_{|n|\leq 1}\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by
Remark

7.29
(1)-(2).

(1)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, by
Remark

7.29
(2) and
[
Nee21a
, Proposition 1.9]
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}_{\operatorname{Qcoh}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i})=0
for all
n+i\geq 1
by
Lemma

7.28
.

(3)
′

By
Remark

7.29
, any
F\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
for
i\geq 0
can be written as a direct sum
F=E\oplus D
with
E\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-i,0]}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{[-i,i]}
and
D\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
. This gives us the triangle
E\to F\to D\to\Sigma E
we needed. For
i<0
, it is trivial, as
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
.

∎

Remark 7.31
.

Let
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
be an affine scheme with a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action such that the corresponding grading on
R
is bounded, that is, only finitely many of the graded pieces are non-zero. Then, the the derived category of the quotient stack
[\operatorname{Spec}(R)/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
can be shown to be
\mathcal{G}
-approximable analogous to the proof of
Theorem

7.30
.

We now give an example of weak
\mathcal{G}
-approximability for a special class of rings. We begin by recalling the definition of a pure-projective module. Pure-projectivity can be defined in a more general setting, but for modules, the following is an equivalent definition.

Definition 7.32
.

Let
R
be a ring. An
R
-module is
pure-projective
if it is the direct summand of a direct sum of finitely presented modules. Given an
R
-module
M
, we define a pure-projective resolution to be a complex
P
of pure-projective modules such that
P^{i}=0
for all
i>0
, which is a
pure exact
resolution of
M
, that is, the complex
\operatorname{Hom}(N,P)
is a resolution of the module
\operatorname{Hom}(N,M)
for all pure projective modules
N
.

The
pure-projective dimension
of a module is the minimal length of a pure-projective resolution.
The
pure global dimension
of a ring
R
is the supremum of the pure-projective dimension of all
R
-modules.

The following result by Kiełpiński and Simson gives a large class of rings with finite pure global dimension.

Theorem 7.33
.

[
KS75
, Theorem 2.2]
For a ring of cardinality less than equal to
\aleph_{n}
, the pure global dimension is less than or equal to
n+1
.

Hypothesis (a)
.

A noetherian ring
R
satisfies
Hypothesis

(a)
if there exists a positive integer
A
such that every
R
-module
M
has a resolution by pure-projective modules of length less than or equal to
A
.

Remark 7.34
.

Note that in
Hypothesis

(a)
, we do not assume that the resolution by pure-projective modules is a pure-exact resolution. Further, note that the
Hypothesis

(a)
is satisfied by a ring if it has finite pure global dimension.

Theorem 7.35
.

Let
R
be commutative noetherian ring satisfying
Hypothesis

(a)
. Then,
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)
is weakly
\mathcal{G}
-approximable (
Definition

4.1
) for the generating sequence (
Definition

3.8
) given by
\mathcal{G}^{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Sigma^{i}M:M\in\operatorname{mod}(R)\}
.

Proof.

We again use the standard co-t-structure
(\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\geq 0},\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\leq 0})
on
\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)
to define an orthogonal metric (
Definition

3.1
). We define the orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
by setting
\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\geq n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Sigma^{-n}\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\geq 0}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. We prove weak
\mathcal{G}
-approximability, see
Remark

4.3
, with this orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
, and the integer
A
given by
Hypothesis

(a)
.

(0)

Note that by definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}
(see
Definition

3.8
(2)),
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\operatorname{smd}(\operatorname{coprod}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,i]))
, see
Notation

2.2
and
Notation

3.6
. Further, from the definition of
\mathcal{G}
, it is clear that that
\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-i]=\{\Sigma^{-n}M:n\geq i\text{ and }M\in\operatorname{mod}(R)\}
. And so, we get that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
.

(1)

Note that
\mathcal{G}(-\infty,-i]=\{\Sigma^{-n}M:n\geq i\text{ and }M\in\operatorname{mod}(R)\}\subseteq\Sigma^{-i}\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}X)^{\geq 0}=\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by definition. And so
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i}=\operatorname{smd}(\operatorname{coprod}(\mathcal{G}(-\infty,i]))\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{G}^{i},\mathcal{R}_{n})=0
for all
i+n\geq 1
using
[
Kra05
, Lemma 2.1]
.

(3)

Let
F\in\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\geq i}\cong\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq i}
. We take its canonical truncation to get the triangle
\tau^{\leq i}F\to F\to\tau^{\geq i+1}F\to\Sigma\tau^{\leq i}F
. Note that this is the required triangle, as
\tau^{\geq 1}F\in\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Qcoh}(X))^{\geq i+1}\cong\mathbf{K}(\operatorname{Inj}\text{-}R)^{\geq i+1}
(
Lemma

7.18
) and
\tau^{\leq i}F\cong\Sigma^{-i}M
for a
R
-module
M
. As
R
satisfies
Hypothesis

(a)
,
M\in\langle G\rangle{[0,A]}\subseteq\langle G\rangle{[-A,A]}
, which gives us that
\tau^{\leq i}F\in\langle G\rangle{[-A-i,A-i]}
, which is what we needed.

∎

8.
Completion and closure of compacts

In this section, we will see what completion à la Neeman gives us for the setting we are interested in, see
Section

2.1
, and in particular
Definition

2.15
. Further, using these computations, we give some applications of the representability theorems proved in
Section

6
.

We start with a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
, and compute what the completion machinery gives us when applied to the category of compacts
\mathsf{T}^{c}
. We recall the relevant definitions again for the reader’s convenience in this setup.

Definition 8.1
.

[
Nee18a
, Definition 1.10]

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a compactly generated triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
). Further, let
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
be a good metric (or equivalently an extended good metric, see
Definition

3.1
) on
\mathsf{T}^{c}
.
We denote the restricted Yoneda functor by
\mathcal{Y}:\mathsf{T}\to\operatorname{Mod}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
, where
\operatorname{Mod}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
denotes
\operatorname{Hom}([\mathsf{T}^{c}]^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Mod}(\mathbb{Z}))
. Then, we can define the following full subcategories of
\operatorname{Mod}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
,

(1)

\mathfrak{L}(\mathsf{T}^{c})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\mathcal{Y}(E_{i}):E_{1}\to E_{2}\to\cdots\text{ is a Cauchy sequence in }\mathsf{T}^{c}\}
, see
Definition

2.14
for the definition of a Cauchy sequence for a triangulated category with a metric.

(2)

\mathfrak{C}(\mathsf{T}^{c})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{M}_{n})^{\perp}
.

(3)

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{T}^{c})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{L}(\mathsf{T}^{c})\cap\mathfrak{C}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
. This is a triangulated category by
[
Nee18a
, Theorem 2.11]

Note that these three categories remain the same if we replace the metric
\mathcal{L}
with an equivalent metric, see
Definition

3.3
.

Recall that
Theorem

2.16
gives us a way of computing the completion of
\mathsf{T}^{c}
. We now compute the categories
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
,
\mathcal{Y}^{-1}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathsf{T}^{c}))
, and
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}(\mathsf{T}^{c})
of
Theorem

2.16
in the setup we are interested in. Recall from
Theorem

2.16
that,

\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\mathsf{T}^{c})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{F\in\mathsf{T}:F\cong\operatornamewithlimits{\underset{\boldsymbol{\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.18501pt}}}{{Hocolim}}}E_{n}\text{ for a Cauchy sequence }E_{1}\to E_{2}\to\cdots\text{ in }\mathsf{T}^{c}\}

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
a triangulated category with a generating sequence
\mathcal{G}
(
Definition

3.8
), and an orthogonal metric
\mathcal{R}
(
Definition

3.1
) such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{G}^{n},\mathcal{R}_{i})=0
for all
i>>0
. If we consider the compacts with respect to the extended good metric
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathsf{T}^{c}
, and the closure of the compacts
\overline{\mathsf{T}^{c}}
(
Definition

3.17
) with the metric
\mathcal{R}
, then
